As it was now quite dark there was no reason for p-stponing her visit; yet she felt a reluctance that was almost a fear to enter by that little dark door 'through which so many miserable Creatures must haw passed, though it is doubtful if any of them felt more miserable than.she.

Knowing she only made her task the heavier by dwelling on it, she hurried to her room, put on a black loose- cloak, and a thick veil, took under her arm the case containing her diamond necklace and went softly out of the house.

• ", On reac unc, the pawnbroker's she looked back- wards and forwards to see that no chance acquaintance was passing, then without further hesitation she hurried np the side street, pushed opened the swing door, and stood at the counter of a dark little shop. When Mr. Lawlor came out from his sitting- room she saw he instantly recognised her, so that she did not attempt to conceal her identity. She was breathing so rapidly that she could hardly get out the words : ‘`l wish—that is I, want—if you will be so kind—Jo get some money on my diamonds."

I was looking for you, Blair, and I hoped I might find you here," he said, hurriedly. "What is it ? " asked Blair, sharply. "I'll tell you as we go to the club," responded the other, who putting his arm within Blair's, pressed it significantly. "Good-night, Lena, I will come and see you to- morrow," said her cousin. Then as the door closed on her he turned to his friend and hurriedly asked: anything wrong? " "My dear fellow, 'there's been a tragedy. That fool, her husband, has killed himself, and I wanted to get here and break the news before the police." "Killed himself ? " said Blair, horror-stricken.

"Yes, the Harpy shut her door on him, it's supposed because he hadn't the money he promised her, and he blew Out the few brains he had on her doorstep." "Good Heavens!" "I pity his wife. You must go back and tell her. It's an ugly job; but you are her cousin and it has to be done. I'm awfully glad I met you for you'll do it fifty times better than me."

HOMELY CHAT ABOUT ACCIDENTS.

Points You Should Note.

Scarcely a day passes without some member of the family getting hurt. Maybe the breadwinner's work makes him peculiarly liable to cuts or lacerations; the boisterous youngsters are always running in to mother with bumps and bruises; and housewor'.: abounds with painful knocks, burns, scalds, and sprains for the fair sex. Prudent is the man who keeps a box of Zartt-Buk handy in his desk-drawer, in his tool-chest, or in his pocket. And far-seeing is the housewife who takes care to keep her home constantly supplied with this pure and.effective first-aid. Promptly dressing an injury with Zam-Buk means speedy relief from smarting pain. That's why the children like it. But it means much more—that your hurt will heal up quickly and perfectly, without fester- ing, without gathering, and withoul harbouring disease germs which, without Zam-Buk, might breed with incredible rapidity and spread pestilence through the tissue. That's why the "grown-ups" like it—why Zam-Buk has been officially adopted by ambulance experts, nurses, and public first-aid organisations. The secret of Zam-Buk's clean and painless healing lies in the fact that it imitates Nature's own " healing by first intention." It is composed of pure vegetable sops and juices, so highly-refined, and so scientifically combined and concentrated, that they sink into every nook and crevice of the wound, as well as through the pores of the skin round about, soothing the pain and allaying inflammation by effective antiseptic and germi- cidal action. Then Zam-Buk .actually helps to knit the flesh together, or to build new cells of healthy skin in the plcce, acting just as. Nature herself would act if the unnatural conditions of modern life didn't prevent her —or if thoughtless people didn't handicap her work by using impure ointments, salves and embrocations made of rancid animal fats and of mineral drugs. Pin your faith to Zam-Buk, the natural healing balm, and keep a box always handy for the accidents that will happen. It costs one-and-three-hallpence or two-and- nine per box, of all chemists, or post free from the Zam Buk Co., 4. Red Cross-street, E.C. The large box con- tains nearly four times as much as the small one.

FLAT-CAPTAIN'S TRAGIC DEATH.

Fall into the Hold.

ALLEGED NON-COMPLIANCE WITH REGULATIONS. -

The tragic death of John Reay,agsad 67, ship's captain, residing at 14, Thewlis-street, was inquired into by Mr. Coroner Brighouse and a jury at the Warrington Police Station on Tuesday. Mr. J. R. Evans represented Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Limited, Mr. E.A.Connor thai deceased's relatives, and Mr. Sumner, Factory Inspector, was also present. The Coroner said the police report stated that the deceased was employed by Messrs. Crosfield and Sons as captain of the flat Fairy. The boat had been loaded and was ready to go up the river to Liverpool at 8 p.m. on Friday last. The flat wiis right alongside the steamer George, and the deck of the latter had to be crossed to reach the Fairy. About seven o'clock Reay saw his son George in Quay Fold, and told him he was going to have his tea on the boat, as his things were there. The son returned to the boat at about eight o'clock, but could not find his father. He then went for his brother Joseph, and together they seaxched and found their father at the bottom of the hold of the George. Dr. Naden was sent for, but Reay was dead, his skull and thigh having been fractured, George Reay, a waterman, son of the deceased, said he last saw his father alive at 7.15 on Friday night,when he said he was going to have his tea on the boat. Witness went home for his tea. His father was strictly sober at the time. Witness arrived back at the boat about eight o'clock, and subsequently found his father at the botton of the hold, dead. In answer to the Coroner,witness said it was possible to step from the quayside on to the bulwark of the George. The George had two hatchways with a passage about four feet six inches batween. Both the hatchways were uncovered, and the hatches were placed in the free passage so that anyone going over the vessel would have to get over them. There were no lights on the quay and it was a very dark night. Questioned by Mr. Evans witness said he did not know it was the duty of the captain of tbe Fairy to move that vessel alongside another steamer which _ _

woUld tow it down the river. _ The Coroner said the law was that if a ship was alongside any other ship in a wharf, and persons employed had to pass from one to another, safe means of access should be provided for their use unless the conditions were such that it wai possible to pass from one to another without undue risk and without the aid of any special appliances. In this case the persons employed on the Fairy had to pass over the George,and there was an obligation on the owners to provide safe means of access. The Fairy had been tied to the George all day, and no one had said a word about it. Nothing . ould be more risky than passing over the George in the dark with both hatchways open and the hatches placed in the free way. A clearer case of irregularity and a greater death trap he could not imagine. Mr. Evans, interrupting, said he could call a witness who would say there was a free way between the hatches. The Coroner said he gave Mr. Evans the opportunity of calling witnesses, but he did not take it. According to the evidence before him the free way was blocked up by the hatches. Thomas Augustus Mosse, manager of the shipping department of Messrs. Crosfield and Sons, was then called. He said he saw the George at nine o'clock the morning after the accident. The hatches were lying between the two hatchways as described, but there was room for a MID to pass between them. The Coroner said it was quite clear the George had to provide the means of access. Nearly all the deaths on board ship were caused through inattention to details, and most of them through hatchways not being properly protected. The hatchways on the George ought never to have been left unprotected. There was no light on either boat or the quay, and when Reay stepped on board he had two gaping holes in front of him which could not be seen in the dark, in addition to which the freeway was either blocked up or partly blocked up. If the hatchways had been protected Reay would not have lost his life. The jury, after a short retirement, returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and added a rider that Reay's death was caused by neligence on the part of the man in charge of the George, the regulations not having been observed. Mr. Evans, on behalf of Messrs. Crosfield, expressed their spepathy with the relatives.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
THAT BRONCHIAL COUGH.

A Tradesman proves the value of the Peps Pine-Air Treatment.

Thee, the Peps pine-air cure for bronchitis is both homely and efficient is proved by the voluntary testi- mony of Mr. Philip Solomon, a well-known Bolton tradesman and very popular in the local lodges of the ft.A.0.8. Interviewed by a pressman, Mr. Solomon said "I have suffered for over fourteen years from bronchitis and bronchial asthma," said Mr. Solomon. "It seemed to get worse and worse every winter, and got so bad at last that I was laid up for over seven weeks and hever expected to get out of bed again. I fancy the trouble was caused by the close confinement of my work as a tailor and cutter. I know I used to feel the cold terribly comi:,g home from the warm workshop at night. "The fits of coughing were so bad, and so frequent, that everybody used to pity me. I could not lie on my back in bed, but had to be propped up to get my breath. My sleep was constantly broken by coughing fits, and T got very weak. "I tried all sorts of things to get relief, but the best of them soon lost their effect, and lett me worse than ever. I was treated by doctors too but with the same result. To one doctor, who is an old friend of mine, I appealed for something that would stop ra) cough. I could stop your cough,' he said, but you would be a dead man in a few days if I did.' His medicine eased me a good bit, but very soon lost its effect, and left me hopeless, ," Then I tried Peps, a new and altogether unique remedy, and the result was beyond all my hopes. I could feel their influence spreading through my lungs and chest at once, quite a different sensation to any other remedy I had ever tried. After taking Peps I could sleep soundly, and my breathing was quite free and easy. At first I feared Peps might serve me the same as other remedies had, and would lose their -effect after a time. But the good effect continued, and seerned to gain rather than lose. Now lam working again as well as ever I did, and I always keep a box of Peps handy in in my vest-pocket, so that whenever Ihe dust of the workshop troubles me, I can get rid of it at once by taking a Pep. The change in me has hem so great that everybody is talking about it, and I have explained so often how the change has come about. that I am a regular walking advertisement for Peps." The lessons of such a chapter of life-history are worthy of serious consideration. Sudden changes from beat to cold, as when leaving a warm room on a cold day, cause bronchitis. Sudden changes in the weather have a similar effect. The wonderful relief afforded by Peps in such a serous case as this proves conclusively the medicinal value of pure pine-sir in the lungs. Such unique effects cannot be produced by ordinary cough- mixtures or by ordinary lozenges compounded of cubebs, ammonia, and other irritating substances. LENA'S HUSBAND.

BY FITZGERALD MOLLOY; Ciathor of "Just at Moonrise,".. "The Mysterious Palace," "Two Women," "Donisthorpe's Destiny," &c.

As Lena sat at breakfast, she was surprised to !war her husband moving abut in the room above, For he seldom rose before noon, and she knew he had not been in until early that morning, for ioon after she had been startled from sleep by the banging of his door, she had heard the clock strike four.

As he entered the breakfast-room she gave him' me of those swift glances by Which women gauge the shifting humours of their lords, and said: "If I had known you were coming down to breakfast," and she looked at the table, on which there was nothing appetising. Then in an apologetic voica she added,"l will ring and see if there is-" "Don't mind, I can't eat anything, but I will have a cup of tea," he answered. She was relieved to find he was neither sulky no silent, nor aggressively irritable, as was usual in the beginning of the day, and her pleasure at the change was so great that she accepted it thankfully without seeking for . its .cause. So with rising spirits the poor little woman ventured to tell him bits of gossip about friends she had seen the previous day, and encouraged by his apparent interest, spoke .of the letter she had that morning from Frank, their only child, a sturdy little lad ft nine, for whom he had never shewn any affection. To part with the boy had been a great grief to her, but as in so many other things she had sacri- ficed herself, believing it w,is better be should be put to school than that he should remain at home where he would .see and resent the unhappiness ,his father caused her. _ _

With glistening eyes- She spoke of the -sco:7B he had made at cricket, his delight, in football, and his love of outdoor sports, which she hoped would please his father, who in his day had been a famous athlete. As she faced him across the breakfast-table she failed to see him as the haggard, dissipated man he was, with eyes bard as flint, and a cruel Month, but imagined him still the gallant, handsome young soldier who, ten years before, hatl wooed and won her, the man for whose sake she had refused her wealthy cousin, Austin Blair, who had loved her all his life, and who had become an exile for years after her marriage, which had been made in opposi- tion to her family and friends. Her husband had long ago left the army, quitting it. soon after she became his wife, with a • sudden- ness that might have roused her suspicion if he had not explained. that his desire was to settle down with her, instead of moving from one garrison town to another with his. regiment. Since then it. had been the endeavour of her life to hide his failings from hes relatives, to deny- to herself that their estimate of him was true ; for in spite of his indifference, his neglebt, his unkindness, she loved him, clung to him, believed in him. His unusually quiet air. and the submissiveness with which he listened to her, at last roused her fears- that. something was wrong with him, and whilst dreading to question him, she ventured to say: "Has anything happened—that is, I hope--" - "Yes ; I'm much upset, Lena," he said, seizing the opportunity he so long expected, "and I haven't slept a wink all night, thinking about it." "What can it be.-?." she asked, under her breath, her gentle eyes full of alarm. "Well, the fact. is, I must have five - hundred pounds by the end of the week." t "Five hundred pounds," she echoed, in dismay. Her whole income barely amounted .to eight. hundred a year, on which they had lived since their marriage, for even when in receipt of his pay, he had never contributed ft penny to the house- hold expenses, whilst she had frequently been obliged to meet his debts. And of late, it had become a struggle to supply him With money, to keep up their unpretending home -and to educate her •boy. Much as she hated- it, site had been obliged to run up bills with the tradespeople.' Long ago she would have gone to the Channel Isles or to the countfy, where they might .econo- mise, but be would not hear of leaving town, where all his pleasures were centred. , "There's no Use in repeating my words," he said, with his old harshneSs, "and you don't seem to care .what I feel in asking you." . "Yes, dear, I do," she replied, conciliatingly, "but it's a large sum, find I owe a good deal to the tradespeople." "For God's sake, don't. worry me with domestic details." • _

"No, but I want you to see how I stand. Then Frank's last term hasn't been paid for " "Of course he's always put before me," he flared out, with the unreasoning jealousy he always shewed towards the lad. "lie's first and. I'm nowhere." -

"Don't be unjust, dear; he must be educated." "Why couldn't he be educated at home and save money? " - She could not explain that she parted with the boy to spare him from wretchedness, but remained silent, the old patient look returning to her face whose outlines had grown so sharp. "I suppose you have lost at cards ? " she suggested, after a pause. -• "Well, what if I have? " he demanded, savagely-. "Only that when I. last paid, you promised me--"-

"I might be sure you would remind me of wha.4 I owe you." "No, it's,nodutt, but " "You need say no more about it ; as you won't give the money, I'll clear out of the country and you'll never be troubled with me again." "0 Harry, how can you say that ?" She almost. cried, her .miserable-, face growing whiter at the thought of losing him. lie knew his threat would have its effect. "There's nothing else for it, unless you can get the money from your trustees," he added, as if by sudden thought. • "It's no use asking thorn ; they won't letomr. have a penny in advance." "There's your cousin who has more coin than he knows what to do with." She had never told him that Austin Blair loved her, and she now believed he had not heard of it from others or he would never have made this suggestion. "I cannot ask him," she. said. "Why not?" "If fonly because he would know I wanted it for you."

"Let him," lie answered, desperately. "Oh, no, no," she said, anxious to spare him the contempt. he invited. "Well, if I don't Mind, you needn't, and all I know that if I haven't it by the end of the week, you won't find me here," saying which he quitted the room. _ _ _ _ _

A. couple of hours later, when he bad dressed with his usual care, he left the house without say- ing a word to her. Pale and miserable she stood at the window by which he passed, and though lie knew she was there he never looked in her direc- tion, but. turned to watch a pretty girl who, with .skirts,, was picking her way across the muddy street. There were salt, tears in Lena's eyes as she hurried to her room that she might think over what she could do. From the first she saw there was but one Way of raising the money, and no doubt lie had , seen it too, but had been too honourable to suggest it.. She must pawn her jewels. - Some of them had belonged to her__ mother. her

dear father had given her the beautiful diamond necklac..., and her bracelets, rings and ornaments had been wedding presents from relatives and friends of her own, her husband's having given

hand-painted frames, volumes of poetry, and s. carriage clock, the joint contribution of three

maiden aunts,

She Aid not know the value of her diamonds. but she was sure old gr. Lawlor, the jeweller and pawnbroker at the corner, would net fairry by her. In days when she had money to spare, she had bought various articles of old silver from him, and she had recently spoken to him about a pair of Georgian candlesticks she had admired in his window, into which she had often cast envious 'ikoks. And more than once she had seen thread- bare figures flit in and out of a swing door in it side street which led to his pawn-office' when she had wondered what necessity had forced them . to pledge their belongings. .That evening, when quite dark, she also would enter by that "Mysterious door. Once within she could not be seen by those passing outside, and old Mr. Lawlor would be discreet, for he looked like the .repository of inmonerable secrets. ' Yet at thought of this visit her cheeks burnt. Then she told herself that whatever her feelings were they must be overcome ; and every sacrifice must be made to keep her husband beside her. Tie did not return to dinner, which was not

St. Peter's Church

The Vicar of St. Peter's, the Rev. B. Percival, entered upon the 15th year of his ministry on Suirday, and in the evening he preached to a large congregation from the words, "With God all things are possible." He remarked that steady progress had been made during the past year in all their parochial organizations, and it was interesting and encouraging to compare the present time with 14 years ago ; in fact, when he looked round the church and saw such a large attendance, it filled his heart with joy to see such a wonderful change. It every branch there had been great and steady progtess—in attendance at evening services, in the number of baptisms, communicants, marriages, finAnces, and last, but not least, in the number of church workers.

Buckley-Street P.S.A.

An open meeting of the Baal,ley-street P.S.A. Society on Sunday was well attended. Mr. E. Flinston occupied the chair. In the absence of Mr.Stringer,who was indis- posed. Mr. Joseph Owen gave an address. Miss Fairhurst was heard to advantage in "The raft," the test piece in the competition which she won at the Warrington Eisteddfod. Mr. A. E. Trow was the accompanist. Mr. Oleave and Mr. H. Woolley also took part in the service.

Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay.

The individual communion cups (presented to the above Mission by Mr. J. J. Crosfield) were used for the first time on Sunday evening last. The Communion Service was conducted by Mr. T. Dale. The impressive- ness of the Service was added to very materially by the fact of all the communicants being able to partake of the Sacrament at one and the same time. The cups and stands have been supplied by the "Onward" Publishing Co. (Band of Hope Union), Deansgate, Manchester, and have given every satisfaction.

1 Many will be pleased to hear that Sergeant Banks, of Newton, has keen made an inspector. Sergeant Ban ks has been in Newton nearly 12 years and is highly esteemed. He has been transferred to the Wigan Dis- L trict.

LIVELY COUNCIL 31./ENES.

COUNCILLOR ASKED TO AP3LIGISE BUT REFUSES.

The Electrical Engineer's Salary.

A low, discussion, marked by a heated incident, took place at meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening. The Electricity and Tramways Committee recommendel that the salary of the Electrical Engineer (Nlr. Mathias) be increased from £3OO to £350 per annum, and that he be allowed to take not more than two pupils at any one time. The MAYoa formally moved and Councillor WILKINSoN seconded the confirmation of the minutes. Councillor STorr thought some reasons ought to be given for the proposed increase of salary. The MAYOR. slid Mr. Mathias was appointed at a very low salary, and he was promised that if he sncceeded in making the electricity works pay he would receive an increase. During the last two years the output at the works had increased over 80 per cent., and there had been a reduc- tion in the price of the current to all classes of con- sumers, yet the profits had been increased very silbstan-- tially. Mr. Mathias had pointed out to them that the— year previous to his appointment the net profit was , £125. The first year after his appointment the profit was £1,540, and this year it was £2,700. He had had all the extension work for the electricity vsorks and tramways, and was at present carrying out a gheme of consiierahle magnitude withoutr anyincrease of staff. The plant, generating station and tramways department were getting older, and required more attention. He was promised an increase if results justified it, and Mr. Mathias contendel that the results could not have been better. He also pointed out that a good many Corporations allowed their engineers to take private pupils, which was not the custom in Warrington. He (the Mayor) had gone over a list of the salaries paid in 30 towns, and found that Warrington paid the lowest. From his (the Mayor's) knowledge Mr. Mathias had saved the town over £3OO in commission alone during the last two years. Previous to his appointment they always hada consulting engineer, and no matter what they bought in the way of cables, &c., a man from London came down to examine it. Mr. Mathias did the whole of that work himself. He was at iiresent, engaged on another scheme. They would have to ex- tendtheir works on account of the large amount of business they were getting, and instead of calling in an engineer Mr. Mathias was getting out the plans and specifications himself and he might save them in this instance £l5O to £2OO. It was not his work to draw the plans, but he was doing it. He (the Mayor) had not the slightest fear in recommending the Council to pass the resolution because he did not think they could get a man to do the work better even if they paid him thousands a year.

A SCENE.

The MAYOR then read a report of how a serious accident was averted at the electric station on Decem- ber 21st through the promptitude and presence of rind of Mr. Mathias and Ips staff. At the close Councillor WILSON rose and said : "A Councillor, here, has been calling out rot. I object to it."

The MANOR. : If you name the gentleman he must pologize.to the chair.

Councillor WlLsos : Councillor Cannington. The MAkOR :, You must apologise, Councillor Cunnington. Councillor CUNNINGTON : What am I going to apologise for? The AfAvon : T have told you. Councillor CUNNINGTON : I will not. The MAYOR: You won't? Councillor CUNNINGTON : No.

The MAYOR : I shall ask you to leave the room, or I can leave the chair.

-60.1-113CI1101 CUN'NINGTON : I have done nothing. Councillor PEMBERTON : I think Councillor Cunning- ton's remark applies not to you, sir,but was his opinion of what took place. Alderman TINNION : It is for Councillor Cunnington to say what he referred to. The action of more than one member of the Council at that end of the room was not creditable to the Council and not respectful to the chair. Undertone conversations and the passing of remarks were going on the whole of the time, and it certainly was disrespectful to the chair. • The MAYOR: Councillor Cunnington will you explain what is "rot "and what is not "rot," in the report?

bouncillor CUNSINGTON : I never said , anything about" rot " at all.

Councillor WILSON: I most distinctly say that Councillor Cunnington used the expression rot." Councillor CUNNINGTON: I distinctly say I never. did. The MAYOR) Very well if ...you deny A. _ _

Councillor Wrtsoti Mr. Mayor, I ask the two councillors sitting on the right hand and the left hand: did they not hear him say it? The .NIAYoR : We cannot go further. He denies it. Councillor PEMBERTON : I heard C4uncillor Cunnington say something, but I did not hear the word. The MAYort : It is very bad and disrespectful to the chair to use such language if it has been said, and I can assure you any such language as that will not be tolerated in this Council while lam in this -chair. I accept his denial, and the matter must go on. Councillor LORD moved an amendment that the minute be referred back. This was a double-barrelled rise. If it we's intended to give Mr. Mathias a rise he should not be allowed to have apprentices which were equivalent to another £5O per annum. The advance of £5O and the permission to take two premium pupils was equal to £lOO a year which was 33 per cent of his salary. Mr. Mathias deserved a good deal of credit for his work, but not the whole of it, as the staff were also worthy of praise. The saving of money was not due to Mr. Mathias alone, but to the cheaper buying of coal. The MAYOR said he quite admitted that the staff deserved credit and he gave it to them. Councillor PEMBERTON seconded the amendment. Mr. Mathias was a good servant, but it should not be overlooked that his salary had been raised in four years from £lO4 to £3OO. He had done very well. Mr. Mathia.s's appointment had placed him in the position of being able to apply to other Corporations for similar positions, as he believed he had done. It was true he did some supervision in connection with the tramway extension, but it was under Messrs. Preece and Cardesv who received a commission and sent down a permanent man. If Mr. Mathias' salary was raised he should give some guarantee that he would spend a certain number of years in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He ought not to be Alowed to take pupils. At this stage there was a "scene " between Councillor Pemberton and Alderman Tinnion,Councillor Pemberton taking the latter to task for holding an alleged-conver- sation across the table with Alderman Evans. He said Alderman Tinnion was so very fond of finding fault with other people that he thought it his duty to object to him.

Alderman TINNION denied talking to Alderman Evans and said it was the alderman who was speaking to him. He objected to Councillor Pemberton's blackguardly way of treating everyone in the Council. Councillor PENIBERToN : I would not be as big a blackguard as you. Councillor EvASS supported the amendment, because he said he had consistently opposed dual appointments. Mr. Mathias was receiving .£5O in connection with the tramway management and £250 as electrical engineer. He hoped the committee would reconsider the advis- ability of ending what he considered to be a dual posi- tion. With a tramway manager like Mr. Telfer he did not see the necessity for an overseer. Alderman FORSHAw said he thought Mr. Mathias was a valuable servant and deserved an increase, but he objected to him taking premiums from pupils. The pupils would be taught at the expense of the Corpora- tion. and the Corporation ought to be entitled to the premiums. In answer to Councillor Stott as to whether any other ohief officials had premium pupils, The MAYOR said the Town Clerk had an articled clerk, and he thought the Borough Surveyor had a pupil. Councillor WILKINSoN denied that Mr. Mathias held a dual position. He was the electrical engineer, and was in command of the electricity works and the over- head equipment. Mr. Telfer was traffic manager solely. Pupils were allowed in other towns, and he thought the idea would be to train up Warrington youths for future high appointments in the borough. Councillor CfrANTLEß,asked if Mr. Mathias would agree to his salary being reduced if there was a loss in the department. (Laughter). When a department made a profit it should be the ratepayers who should benefit, and not the officials. (Hear, hear). The department was not in a hurry to advance the wages of the poorly. paid servants. It saas more difficult for a lower paid employee to get an increase of Is.than for a high placed official to get £l. Councillor STorr said his views were somewhat in accord with those which had been expressed by the last speaker, but though workingmen should have con- sideration he did not think they should penalize an official because certain members of the Council did not treat the ordinary working man as fairly as he ought to be treated. That was not the fault of the chief officials, and they ought to consider questions of this kind from a single standpoint without bringing in side issues. If the committee would come before the Council and recommend an increase of £5O he would support it, but he did not feel inclined to agree to the pupils as well. If premiums were charged it would debar the children of workingmen from securing the good positions. Alderman EVANS explained that the suggestion as to the pupils was made by a member of the committee, and Mr. Mathias was in no way responsible/ , Councillor Fur:l'm-Small said Councillor Stott had apparently forgotten one of the trade unions the Coopers were allowed to take on apprentices. The first year, whatever money the paid was given to the foreman and not to the firm. The second year, a proportion went to the firm, but the bulk went to the foreman, and it was the same with an electrical engineer, who was supposed to give the benefit of his experience to his pupils, whilst the pupils were giving the benefit of their

wotc to thair ernplayers; so- it cut both ways, and' enibled the employers to get labour cheap. The MAYOR said he had explained to Mr. Mathias. that if the resolution was passed ho • would. have to be content for a good number of years. WARRINGTON ANGLERS' ASSOCIATION,

A Disappointing Season.

Although the Warrington Anglers Association main- tains its sound position, the past season was one of the most disappointing over experienced—through circum- stances beyond the control of the Association. There is, however, no diminution of interest on the part of the members, and there was a large attendance at the annual meeting of the Association held at the Norton Arms Hotel on Wednesday evening. Mr. J. Lyon, Whittle had been 3nnounced to preside but wrote regretting his inability to be present and wishing the members a happy and prosperous New Year and a successsul angling season. Mr. Henry Taylor was elected to the chair and amongst those present were: Messrs. W. A. Proud, A. Cook, P. Knight (general secretary), and F. J. E mson (assistant secretary),

THE REPORT. Mr. PETER KNIGHT (secretary) presented his annual report, in which he emphasised the fact that their position, numerically and financially, had been well maintained. They had a large number of defaulters, but the amount owing was considerably less than last year. The collection of subscriptionn by secretaries of clubs had resulted in a better return, though one or two clubs had failed to earn the premium otiercod. The sum of £2 17s. Gcl. had been handed to the sumessful as commission.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
POOR SPORT.

In dealing with the question of private waters, the- SECRETARY said they zegretted to find they had not yielded the sport they expected after the extensive stocking, but when they analysed the angling season all round they found they had no more grounds of com— plaint than other people had. The season ;had been about the worst which had been experienced for ten years, the heavy decrease in the aggregate of the fish weighed in from both home and foreign waters proving that quality and quantity were below the ordinary standard. But it was a condition of things they believed would be improved upon during the ensuing year.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKING.

Stocking had been very extensively carried out during the past year, the Bridgewater Canal receiving the bulk (over 5,000) and the Old Quay Canal about 2,000.

THE BOLLIN.

The 13ollin had disappointed everyone, as only two specimens had resulted from the efforts of visitors, and and these were far below the standard of former years. It had been decided that the annual charge of that fishery far outweighed tho results and that some retrenchment must take place. It had been resolved to dispense with the service of a regular keeper. Any member wishing to fish the Bollin must now confine his efforts to the land under the control of Mr. S. Ileathcote on the Lymm side of the lower length, and the upper length might be fished on the Dunham side from Bollington Mill down to the bridge or the-eabouts, and on the Lymin side from Bollington Mill down to the railway. A proper delineation of the boundary would be notified in due course when the pending negotiations were concluded. The members might rely upon them retaining as much of the fishing as possible consistent with its actual value.

OTHER WATERS.

The length of the Gowy taken over by them during the year had, like many other waters, fished very badly. The Llangollen fishery had experienced a very slack time, but though the catches had been considerably smaller some very good specimens had found their way to SVarrington.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTESTS &C.

The angling contests had passed off very successfully though the number .of entries had not been quite so good as last year. The amounts realised from entrance. fees, with the grants from the funds, and_specimen prizes, came to the fine aggregate of £6B 103., distri- buted among 110 prize winners, The shield and medal competition was a successful event, though the entries were slightly less, there being 27 teams and 32 indi- viduals, a total of 196 competitors. The total value of the medals presented by their esteemed chairman of the Association, together with 10:3. 2d. in cash, was £2O. The weights were much in excess of last year. The competitions for the ensuing season would be placed later in the season to enable the committee to try an experinient, namely ta permit the use of bait cans in all the contests but only on condition thit they were not less than 9 inches by G inches and 5 inches deep, and that undersized fish only were kept in them. It had been decided to throw the length of the Bridgewater canal open for the use- of feeders, in competitions and in ordinary angling,with the exception of the length between Moore Bridge and Keckwick Bridge, which was reserved absolutely for soft ground bait.

The total receipts were somewhat less from several causes, but they had been enabled to spend a fair sum in stocking, and had acquired the additional fishing of about five miles of the Sankey canal. They had enrolled during the year about 220 members as members of the Widnes Msociation to fish the lowet part of the, same water, and with that additional expenditure they were. enabled to carry forward a balance of over £l2 to next year's account. They claimed, therefore, that the committee were carefully considering the best interests of the Association. The Secretary in conclusion, appealed on behalf of the committee for coutinued support with the assurance that the best interests of the members would be safely guarded. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19070112_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====

The balance shoot showed that the receipts amounted to £261 O. 31d. and after paying expenses there was a. credit balance of L'l2 3s. 7.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Abbey ,Fnneral.—lt is stated that the funeral of Lady Burdett-Coutts at Westminster Abbey afforded the first instance of the interment of a woman within the Abbey by virtue of her own merit.- There is an analogous case in that of Mrs. Elizabeth Fry, but she was buried at St. Paursancl without any very exhaustive examination it would appear to he the fact that the Baroness Burdett-Coutts is the only woman to whom burial within the Abbey has been accorded except as a recognition of husband or father's rank, or public services, or on account of her exalted position as queen or memher of a Royal family. There is another woman still among us, upon whom a similar honour may possibly . be conferred, hut it is meet and right that the lady for ' whom a precedent should have been made was the dis- tinguished philanthropist whose benevolence found objects for its exercise in every part of the world. It is too, singularly appropriate that the remains of Lady Burdett-Coutts should lie beneath the shadow of the statue of the great Earl of Shaftesbury, of whom it is ' said that he was" endeared to his conntrymen by a long life spent in the cause of the helpless and suffering." ! Lord Shaftesbury was one of Lady Barr eft-Caitts' most ' valued friends, and it was she aaho unveiled the statue to his memory. Factory Workers' Dress.—Owing to influenza being epidemic in Preston, the Medical dfficer of Health has advised women workers to wear clogs and shawls, on leaving the mill: instead of "brown-paper shoes, and last summer's hats."—The advice may be good from the point of view of utility, although it is fairly well estab- lished that influenza is much more frequently contracted through defective ventilation, than exposure to cold. The doctor would probably say that the aesthetic aspect is of no importance in comparison -with the preservation of healtl-,-hut still it must be admitted that the clogs and woollen shawls whick are worn over the head tn so many industrial towns of Yorkshire and Lancashire. are by no means beautiful, and that in many other districts the women and girl workers would not consent to wear them for any consideration. The Northern factory worker might say with truth that the shawls and clogs are more becoming than the ordinary attire of a frictory girl in the east end of London, and that while clogs iceep the feet dry, the shawls are not only warm but very convenient, seeing that no time is wasted in putting them on or off. At the same time it is quite possible that there is a third course open, and that some service would be rendered to the community by any person who could devise a _dress for women workers which would be warm, comfortable, and at the genie time becoming. The Hot-Water Cure for Inflnenza.—Those who like Mr. J. R. Sims may have suffered from their hundred and forty-second attack of influenza, might well try the simple remedy advocated by Dr. G. S. Keith, of Edin- burgh. This is simply to go to bed, take no solid food, and drink only very hot water. it is said by many who have tried all kinds of treatment that this is the most speedy and effective cure. It has been recommended to Mr. Balfour, who has a painfully familiar acquaintance with influenza, and to this treatment he attributes his more than usually rapid recovery from his last attack. Games for Girls.—The discussion on " Games for Girls" at the Teachers' Conference, was not remarkable for illuminating ideas, but it pointed the moral that much may be done by organised effort. to help children in their school-gam. -Dy sun means the timid child, and the boisterous dominee.ingipone, were brought together upon more level terms, with advantage to both, and moreover it was possible to infuse into the games, more of the educational and elevating character than was the case if the children were -left to their own devices for amusement. For the rest we do not appear to be in any danger of neglect of games either for boys or girls. In fact, many parents would be inclined to sympathise with the mother who wrote to the teacher to ask that haa- daughter should not be taught so many skipping and other exercises because it wore out her boots, and the " oilcloth in the passage," and "made her eat something awful." In this we have gone from one extreme to another. Formerly girls were no sooner in their teens than the only exercise permitted them at school was the two and two parade, or" crocodile walk," as it is termed in the more figurative language of the present day. But nowadays, with the exception of football,girls indulge in all their brothers' games and on the whole they are the better for it. The danger is rather one of excess than of neglect, but the Conference will have done some good if it helps to promote the happy medium in such things. Society Weddings.—There is no doubt about a train of bridesmaids being a much more expensive affair to the bridegroom during the Tinter months, than at any other season of the year for at winter weddings the happy man is expected to present each" maid "with a muff of sable, ermine, or chinchilla, instead of the usual brooch or bracelet, and bouquet, deemed accept- able at other times. During the present month, over one hundred and fifty society weddings are to take place in London alone, and the dressmakere, jewellers, and florists, are reaping a rich harvest. Freaks of Fashion.—Coloured diamonds are the craze of the moment and in the jewellers' shops may be seen grey, green mauve, blue brown black, and yellow

diamonds, mounted in every conceivable way. Many coloured pearls too, are very fashionable and one well- known Society woman is rarely seen without a necklace composed of pink, grey, bronze, black, mauve, and yellow pearls. Black pearls are greatly sought after, but as they baao ne more and M 9113 rare so the price becomes more and more prohibitive. Sashes in Favour.--The sash is a favourite finish to young girls' airy-fairy dance dresses,soft Oriental satin being the fabric preferred for it. To be correct the sash should be caught somewhat low in front, with a pretty clasp, or brooch, and instead of a bow, at the back, the ends should he drawn through a small wreath of flowers, similar to the one destined to be worn in the hair. The long ends should then bh loosely knotted, and allowed to fall to the hem of the skirt.

ed Lace.—Dyed lace is as fashionable as ever,and many early spring toilettes shew a trimming of this description. Moreover the finer and more cobwebby the material of which the gown is built, the heavier as a rule, is the lace, a notion taken from last autumn's mode of bordering dresses of crêpe de chine, voile, and all similar fabric with bands and appliqutSs of velvet. A light weight fabric hemmed with a heavier one, makes generally for a graceful skirt, but one must exercise care that the bordering is not too heavy, or after a few times wearing, it will break away from the skirt fabric, a procedure which, needless to say, will spell complete disaster. Table Decoration.—Now that snowdrops and violets are making their appearance, it is as well to con- template the best methods whereby we may utilise these delicate spring blossoms for table decoration. At many of the large shops, little wheelbarrows in rush work, either natural or silvered over, are in evidence in the fancy departments and these filled with violets, or snowdrops—a layer of damp moss being arranged underneath—look particularly well. Little sacks of white sAtin too, lined with waterproof sheeting, and with a layer of wet moss at the bottom, make the prettiest possible receptacles for violets and snowdr ops. \Allen filled, two or three sacks should be tied together, with white or mauve ribbon, as the case may be. Another charming notion is to get a log—which should be covered with lichen, out of the woods, or from a florist. The centre shmuld then be hollowed out, filled with damp moss, and the violets and snowdrops arranged in clusters. Trails of small ivy over and aroma' the log, add to the beauty of this unique table decoration. _ _ .

Michael Prendergast, who was remanded to the Workhouse by the Manchester Bench on Wednesday, is under the impression that he is the King of England. He stopped people in Corporation-street to communicate the strange information, and added that he wou Id be crowned on March Bth next. BY ALICE MAUD MEADOWS,

Atthor of "One Life Between," "A Million oi Money," "The Extreme Penalty," "For LOTS of a Lady," "I Vargo You Both," &o.

CnAPTER THE CHIEF WARDER ON THE TRACK.

A man with a good joke---or what he considers 7% good joke against 'someone else, which circumstances compel him to keep to himself, is very often an annoying party. • Warder Sullivan was certainly a thorn in the flesh of Chief Warder Clark; he was always looking at him with a broad grin on his face; sometimes- ho burst out laughing for apparently no reason. As the chief warder had no clue to the mystery, it riled him considerably. So much so that more than once the men nearly came to blows.

"Look here ! " he said one day. "I don't know Ivhat you're always grinning and laughing at me for. If you're mad you ought to be locked up in a lunatic asylum; and if you're not you ought to go to prison for wilfully annoying and provoking 11, fellow-creature to violence. You've got some sort of joke, I suppose; well, let's hear it, and have done with it."

The two men were walking side by side; the one angry, the other laughing inwardly. "I should have thought, being the seventh son or the seventh child of a seventh child, and possessing the gift of second sight, you'd have been able to look into my mind and see what I'm laughing at," the other returned, "just as you were able to look into the safe and see who was hidden there. It's too funny!" He burst out laughing as he spoke—roared with laughter. Chief Warder Clark grew more nettled than ever.

"Well, if it wasn't a woman who had been hidden there, what was it ? " heosaid. "Have you any better explanation of the .blue funk he was in? You saw him trembling from head to foot, white as a ghost, terrified half out of his Wits— and then there was the handkerchief. Why, I'd have reckoned things up all right even without the gift of second sight! However, that's nothing to do with the question I asked you. I want to know why you always grin when you look at me? As for looking 'into your mind, you mustn't expect me to perform the impossible; if you had any mind I could see into it quick enough." Warder Sullivan looked vexed. "You needn't be rude," he said, huffily. "As for answering your question I decline to do so; I've a perfect right to look at you and roar with laughter if like. Perhaps something in your face amuser me. As for you, if you're so mighty clever, why don't you locate No. 27? Your gift of second sight would be worth something then. Well, our ways lie apart here. Good-day to you." - He laughed as he walked off, swinging the little basket in which his wife always, put him up some refreshments.. He had had hard work not to tell her all he knew. Human beings are So constituted that most find it very diffi2ult to keep anything to themselves. However, he had succeeded, though he often ached to impart the information—she would have opened her pretty eyes so wide. Chief Warder Clark walked on slowly, thinking to himself, wondering what the joke was Sullivan had against him. He had only grinned like this since the morning after the convict had escaped— was it possible he knew anythirw of the where- abouts of the convict? Was thate.the joke? "He was always partial to Gentleman Jim," he said to himself, always ..favoured him; and yet I don't think he would have connived at his escape, or help-to hide him after he had escaped, he's too practical a man for that, his place means too much, to him. Still, he might know something without having aided and abetted. I'll keep my eye on him." He walked on slowly, and after a time a boy overtook him. He carried- a square, covered basket in one hand, swinging it at his side. The chief warder knew him quite well, it was Mr. Beatty, the chemist's, boy. "Hullo, Bill," he said, "where are you off to ? I didn't know there were any houses in this direction."

The boy moderated the pace at which he had been walking. "There's old Dignal's cottage," he said. "The poor old chap's been ill fonasomo days; came down to our place for medicine pretty. well every day—yesterday and to-day he didn't come, so I've taken it upon myself to go and look him up, to see if there's anything I can do for him or his pretty daughter. You knew, didn't you, that he had a pretty daugirjter ? " Chief Warder Clark shook his head.

"Well, he has," the boy said—"as pretty a one as you'll find in a day's march. If I was a bit older, and in better circumstances, there's no knowing what might not happen. If you've got nothing better to do you might come with me, just to see what you think of my taste." Clark laughed. "All right, youngster," he said, "1 wanted a bit of a stretch; but you're too young to think of sweethearts." They walked on, talking of one thing a`nd another. Of the escape of the convict; the wedding of John Brandon and Celia Verde, and gossip they had heard. The boy liked the company of the prison warder; felt rather grand to be walking with him. When they reached the cottage where Dignal they paused a moment before they knocked. It all seemed very silent; not a sound was to be heard. Was it possible anything had happened to the old man? And then, all, of,a sudden, they heard a man's voice—not the voice of old Dignal, though it was eomplaining, querulous. "I 'want to know where I am," it said—"where I am; and who brought me here. I don't know anything, and I don't know you." Chief Warder Clark started, and looked at the boy strangely. A tingling of excitement went all through him.

"Yo-u are in Dignal's cottage," came the answer in a girl's soft voice. "You have hurt yourself, and we are taking care of you—you'll soon be well now."

Warder Clark said "Hush!" to the boy, crept along the side of the cottage -until he came to the window, and tried to look in; this, however, he found impossible to do, but he heard the man speak again. . "How did I hurt myself? I can't remember hurting myself." "And I don't know how you did it," she answered, "I haven't asked any questions. Father says you are his brother; if that's so, you must be my uncle, but you seemed too young—you--" A sharp tug at Warder Clark's arm drugged b;im away from the window. Bill glared at -him with flashing eyes. "What are you prying into ? " ha said. "What right have you to go -eavesdropping under a window? I wouldn't have brought you here if I'd thought you were that kind of man; come away. If you want to see into the house, go. through the door, like an honest man ! " Chief Warder Clark laughed. "They wouldn't ; let'me," he answered. "DO you know who they've ' got in there? Can you keep a secret—you must keep this one, any way. They've got No. 27, Gentleman Jim, him who escaped from the prison the .other night. I'm sure of it, certain —just as sure as though there had been no I curtain to the window. I've got the gift of , second sight, and I see things Ahers don't. I heard his voice too—a slow, gentlemanly voice. I suppose, he hurt himself trying to get away, and old Dignal is harbouring him for the sake of what he'll get—he'll be lucky if it's not a few years' penal for himself!" , The boy's face fell. "I don't believe it," he said, "and if it is so, it's my fault you'*e found out, and you'll be a sneak if you tell. Any way, I shall warn them; I'm fond of her." "You won't do anything of the liind ! " the man answered, and, catching the boy's arm, began to drag him away. "You hold your tongue. And you need not think "—this seemed to him an occasion upon which one might be excused for liying—"you need not think I hadn't, my sus- picions. I had. I was on my way here when you overtook me. The old man hasn't been ill at all, he was only pretending. The convict is the sick man—he'll be sicker before we have done with him; and you, too, if yon don't mind what you're up to. Why, do you know what would happen to you if you went against the Govern- -ut and warned this man ?"

The bcy gave a twist in the man's hands, but he did not get free.

"You'd get locked up, certain sure," Warder Clark said; "perhaps for ten years. Half a century ago you'd have been hanged for such au

airair—it's treason. , Now, you keep quiet, and I'll see you don't lose by it. 1 should like tc have all the _honour and glory of catching thif man."

The by continued to struggle,'" )-_.:4t Warder Clark led him onward, not relaxing his grasp in the least.

"You'd better make sure 110 is your man first," the boy said. "You'd leek foolith if he 'isn't. Let me go, will you. - If you don't I'll.kick ! " "If you kick, I'll cuff, Clark 'answered, "so look out. Don't be a foolish boy. Here you've a chance of distinguishing yourself—at least, I don't quite mean that, because; of couise, all the credit of finding this man is due to me. But if you'll hold your tongue, I'm your fr?end for life. You're, fit for something better 'than to be a chemist's boy. You ought to get on in the world, as I have, and you would if you had someone to help you a little—push, you ea! Now, you hold .your tongue for a day or two in ty take me all that time to make assurar.,!e doubly sure—and when you're old enough I'll get you on the prison staff. How would you like that, eh ? " He spoke affably, persuasively; he laughed pleasantly. The boy's eyes shone his colour rose; it, had always seemed to him to be a grand thing to be a prison official, to have the right tt. go in and out of. the prison, to see the prisoners every day. Some of the men, he knew, at times, when the convicts worked outside the walls, stood over them with loaded guns; had the right to fire at them as one would at a wild beast if they tried to get away. That see;l.ed a,glorious thing! He was certainly,tempted. But he thought of the pretty girl and the old flther: If he kept silence he would get them both into trouble. Perhaps they would have to go to prison. sit was not to he thought of! He was just thinking of refusing, When it suddenly occurred to him that even if he promised he need not perform. If he didn't promise perhaps he would be locked up, and all chance of warning the Dignals would be gone. A slow smile crossed his face. Warder Clark had noted his rising colour, his shining eyes. Ho bAieved the boy would do as he wished.

"All right," he said, "I'll hold my tongue. But you won't he harder on them—l mean the old man and his daughter—than you can help, will you E. Perhaps they don't know the man's a convict."

Wardel-. Clark released the boy, but watched him narrowly. "I'll see everyone's let down lightly," he answered. "As you say, likely as not they don't know the man is a convict. I'm ping to give you a "shilling now, my boy, and that'll be only the beginning of all I'm going to do for you." The boy, took the coin, smiled, and slipped it into his pocket. "And you won't forget about ei2eing I'm made a warder when I get older?" he said: "I won't forget," Warder Clark answered. "You keep your mouth shut, and this will be the best day of your life—the very best ! " They walked back to the village, chatting pleasantly, and parted there. The boy made a great show of going towards his home but as soon as he was out of the sight of Warder Clark -he doubled back, and was soon hastening at his top speed towards Dignal's cottage. Like most boys who read a lot of rubbishing literature, a convict was a sort of hero to him. Why, Jack Sheppard and Claud Duval were prismiers once—splendid fellows like' that, chaps that were afraid of nothing! . This man might be, probably ,was, a hero too. He had escaped from prison, and Shouldn't be re-taken if Bill could help it. Besides, if he were taken at the cottage of Mr. Dignal it would injure pretty Ruth. The thing was not to be thought of. _ _ . . _

He took a different route from that he had travelled before that day, hiding behind trees as much as possible. He had no fear that he was being followed, but to pretend that he was probably gave an added zest to the adventure. When he reached. the cottage he was very hot, very breathless. He knocked softly, so softly that he had to repeat it. Then he heard a light footfall; his boyish heayt beat very loudly—she was coming! She opened the door • slowly, not very wide. Bill put his hand on his lips. "Hush!" he said, in a dramatic whisper. "Let me in—all is not lost; there is yet time. I left the minion of the law. far away, but not a moment must be wasted!"

"Why, Bill,"

She looked at him and laughed. she said, "have you gone silly ? " He shook his head. "Let me in," he said again. "I tell you I know all; this isn't the first time I have been here to-day. What makes your father do such a thing? It's dangerous— dangerous. Everyone heard the guns on Thursday night, everyone will suspect." Some glimmer of the truth came to her; sbe laughed quietly. "Come in, you silly boy," she said, "but don't make a noise. We've a sick man here, though you need not talk about it. Father, for some reason or other, wants the thing kept quiet. I know I can trust you." Ile slipped into the tiny passage, hat in hand, and shut the door softly. "You might trust me with your life," he awiwered. "You know, of course, that I love you, though I don't expect any return. only a boy earning five shillings a week. I couldn't ask a woman to share that, especially as mother takes four shillings. Miss Dignal, as I look at you it seams to me I read truth in your eyes. Is it possible you are in the dark—that you don't know who the sick man is ? "

She nodded, smiling pleasantly. she returned. "Do you 9" Ho came nearer to her ; he spoke in a very low, stage whisper. "Yes, he said, "I know. Are we quite alone? Is there anyone near who can hear 9" "No one," she answered. "Father had to go to the next village to-day: you and I are quite alone, excepting for the sick man."

"Quite true,"

They had entered the little parlour now; the room in which the sick man lay had once been a part of it, and been partitioned off—the partition did not reach the ceiling. As Ruth spoke, the invalid opened his eyes, looked about him wonderingly, as he always did; seemed worried and puzzled; sat up. He was lying on a bel, fully dressed; he had, just before lie dropped off into a drowsy sleep, stated his intention of going out. Ruth had dissuaded him; had begged him to wait until her father returned, and he had consented. Now he forgot all about that—only felt he wanted exercise.

Bill perched himself on the table, and glanced towards the partition. "He's ,in there ?". Bill ,said i the question an 63sertion. ye The sick man lifted his head, and listened. "You've been- sitting with him, perhaps looking after him ? " "Yes." , The boy dropped his voice, but his words were still quite audible. - "You wouldn't have had you known who he is," he said. "You'd have been frightened out of your life. You know a pri,soner escaped from Dartmoor last night? • - - _

The girl grew- pale. A strange look flashed across the face of the man who listened—a waiting, anxious horrified look. - -•- "Of course," :he returned; "I heard the guns. What of that 9"

Bill swung his legs carelessly. "Oh, only that the man in the next room is he! Don't scream, there's nothing to scream about—l'm here to look after you. For some reason or other your father is harbouring him. Perhaps he hag. wr.""

0 r filen ws, I L ; -a pcooLL,.., on some business of his that-he's away to-day. Well, I've come to warn you. (inc of the warders at Dartmoor knows the mini's here; he'll probably be at this cottap Eome time to-night, and take him. It's a pity yourlather isn't at home to tell you what,to do; you don't want the poor fellow taken, of course:" The man in the next room leant forward, a .strange, strained look upon his face. Was that the truth? he wondered. The back of his head had been injured, ached horribly, be couldn't remember anything—hut it seemed impossible he could be a convict. And yet, why not? He couldn't say just now what he was, or had beer. He looked at his hands; they were bronzed, firm, almost hard, but they did not look like hande that had worked greatly. He put his hand up to his head, wincing as he touched the back. The hair was certainly very short. 'Did that mean what the boy said .was -true? That he had escaped from a convict prison? Was he in danger of being re-taken? - "I won't be ! " he said. "I won't be! I'll have a fight for it. If I've gained my liberty I'll keep it, but I don't remember. It will come back to we, of course, but I don't remember now." Me heard the girl speaking; her voice trembled. 001 a, what ehall we do!" she said. "No, he must not be taken; father would .never forgive • rue. He must have his reason, of course, tor acting as he does; and yet, if I tell ~this man, I don't think he'll understand. He's ill, silly in a way, poor fellow, and I can't believe he's what you say—he doesn't look like it." Bill leant very near to her. "Isn't he wearing the clothes?" he asked. "You know, the broad arrow clothes?"

"No," she said, quickly, "he isn't." The boy drew a deep breath. "That makes things easier," he said; "but he has had them, you may depend upon that. Your father .has probably, got him others—l expect he's been paid over and over again for them. Well, silly or no, you must get him away; there's no doubt he's the man—none whatever. It's perfectly well known that the escaped convict is still hiding in the neighbourhood; everyone is on the watch for him' and now orie man knows he is here. What sort of a looking chap is he ? " _ _

She coloured 'faintly; she could not have told why. The boy's eyes darkened with jealousy. "Very handsome," she said—"very, very hand- some, and he talks like a gentleman. Shall I have to tell him he's found out? Poor fellow! I left him sleeping like a baby." A For all that; he was very wide awake now, and trembled like a leaf. He was so in the dark, his brain had not yet recovered from the blow his brother had given him. He heard, and could not say it was not true, that he was an escaped convict; that the police were searching for him; that someone was coming to that very cottage to take him back to prison—to prison which he could not in the least remember, but which was, he was sure, a terrible, terrible place. " "You must wake him up," the boy said. "You must make him- understand he's got to be got away. I'll help you all I can. l'll"—he hesitated an instant—"l'll even take him somewhere and hide him, if you'll say I may dolhat for your sake; and it will be for your sake, because if he's found here I expect both I and your father Will be sent to prison!" The man behind the partition rose. He must get away; he was injuring, putting in danger, these folk who for some reason or other had sheltered him. He put his hand in his pocket ansl took out a handful of money, gold and silver, then a note. He looked round for his hat, and very silently, very stealthily, he crept away. When Ruth and Bill at length looked into the room, it was empty. Pinned to the quilt was a banknote value ten pounds; otherwise there was no sign of the sick man. "Gone!" Ruth said.

"Gone!" the boy repeated. "He heard our conversation, of course, and has stolen off. Well, after all, it's the best thing he could have done. This note, and his, stealing away, is proof positive that he is what Warder Clark thought him—No. 27, the escaped convict ! " The girl covered her eyes with her hands. "I don't believe it ! " she said, and burst into tears. "Oh, let us go and look for him ! Poor fellow! poor fellow! He's ill —who knows what may happen to him ? " Bill's rather heavy brows drew together. "He won't mind what happens so long as they don't take him," he answered. "Ruth "—he caught her hand—"l believe you've fallen in love with him ! " She looked up defiantly, her face rosy red, her eyes shining through her tears. "You believe," she answered. "Well "—she threw up her head—"you're welcome to believe what you like. As for me "—she laughed excitedly —"I know I have, and they shan't take him— whatever he has done, they shan't take him!"

CHAPTER XXII. WARDER CLARK ON THE WARPATH.

After Bill had left him, Chief Warder Clark walked along, thinking deeply. He had located the convict;, the man was ill; he could take him whenever he liked. Honour and glory, promo- tion and reward would be his; they would also be dealt out to anyone whom he should get to help him. Human nature is proverbially selfish; to be able to say "Alone I did it "is the desire of most. Whatever honour and glory, promotion' and reward was going Warder Clark thought he should best like to keep to himself. "No one ever gave me a leg up," he said to himself; "why should I help others? No, I'll take this man alone, or with a little unofficial help:. What's the good of being specially equipped by nature, as I am, with the gift of second sight, if I share my knowledge with others ? "

_ He had no doubt of the bonafides of the boy, Bill's, promise; the romance of a convict prison seemed something stupendous to hint. He wanted to be a warder; perhaps, in time, if he was very lucky, a chief warder. He would hold his tongue. Besides, having gained one shilling, he would, of course, want more. "This is a bit of business that has got to be very carefully handled," Warder Clark said to himself. "Theis many as couldn't handle it, because it requires brains, but perhaps there is one as can. He spent his off-time walking about and think- ing, then he went back to the prison. At six o'clock he was off duty. ."I'll go to the Pig and Whistle," he said to himself; 'there:ll be plenty'of yokels there only too ready and willing to earn a shilling, if there are any knocking about. They shan't know a word about, my find until I march Gentleman Jim back, to the prison. I'll take my pistol in case he . shews fight. if he does, • I'm perfectly justified in shooting." He made his way to the licensed house in question. A number of men were sitting there smoking and drinking; he looked at each with what he considered a penetrating, second-sight look, choosing his instruments. Then, having made his selection, he spoke in a whisper to four of them—would they care to earn a couple of shillings? Naturally, being fond e beer and tobacco, they would. if so, they were to meet him at a particular spot at nine o'clock. As chance had it it was a dark night. Chief Warder Clark was first at the place of rendezvous, but the others turned up with, praiseworthy punctuality. Chief Warder Clark swore them to secrecy— which, considering they were far away from anyone else, was not absolutely necessary—then imparted his information. "Remember," he said, "he's a desperate man. don't suppose for a moment he'll have firearms, though he may—it would be possible, of course— but he'll fight for his life. We must creep up to the house; while you watch the windows, I'll enter by the door. If you hear me whistle come to my assistance; if not, stand by." "All right, Captain," one of the men answered, with a grin. "If there's any danger you'll let us know; if there ain't,, if he'll conic like a lamb, you'll handle the job alone." Warder Clark frowned. "No Baum!" he said. "This is to be a one-hand job, if human pluck and nerve can make it so; if it can't, I'm not going to attempt the impossible. Fall in there— mark time—quick march ! " He .put himself at their head, and two and two, laughing and jeering under their breath, as perhaps was only natural, they followed. "Should say it's a mare's nest, shouldn't you ? " one asked another. "If Warder Clark has located the escaped convict, it's the first thing he ever has located." .

"Gxeept the Pig and Whistle," the other returned. And with a loud guffaw the two men enjoyed the splendid joke. Warder Clark looked round. "Silence there i ne said in a voice of authority. "Silence, for your lives! You don't know the man as I do. Tread softly, and bridle your tongues, or it may be the worse for you!" The night was very dark. As they walked along they passed a man who crouched behind a hedge —a man who knew he was frightened, whp knew he must hide, but had forgotten-why. THE DOCTOR S RECIPE.

Currant bread should always contain a sufficiency of fruit. Sir Francis Laking recommends the combination of one part of currants with three parts of dough ; these proportions give us a bread liberally enriched with currants and in every way calculated to promote health and aid digestion. Currant bread should be thinly cut ; by this means the fruit is sliced through, and the bread is not only more deliciouily flivoured but also looks much nicer than when cut thick.

WEDDING OF MRS. H. BROADBENT.'

There was t large and fashionable assembly on Satur- day afternoon at the handsome church of All Saints', Enmsmore gardens, London, when Mr. Arthur Henry Dillon, elder son of the late Hon. Conrad Dillon, add nephew of Viscount Dillon, of Ditchley, Oxfordshire, was married to Mrs. Hilda Broadbent, widow of the late Mr. Charles • Harold Broadbent, J.P., of Upton Hayes, Chester, and daughter of the Right Hon. Sir John Brunner, Bart., P.C., M.P. for the Northwich Division of Cheshire, of Druids Cross, Wavertree, Liverpool; Winnington Hall, North wich, Cheshire; and 9, Ennismore-gardens, London, S.W. The service was semi-choral, and the chancel was arranged with tall palms and white flowering plants. The ceremony was conducted by the Rev. Francis Dillon, vicar of Tulney, assisted by the Rector of All Saints' Church. The bride, who arrived in good time, was accompanied by Sir John Brunner. who, during the singing of a nuptial hymn conducted her to the chhncel entrance and then gave her away. She was much admired in a gown of very pale grey cloth ornamented with lace, and grey hat trimmed with grey ostrich feathers. She wore no ornaments, and looked stately walking up the aisle of one of the most artistically decorated churches of London. Captain Eric Dillon (brother of the bride- groom) acted as best man. There were neither brides- maids nor pages. Among those present at the church were Viscount and Vicountess Dillon, Sir George and Lady Mary Dashwood, Miss Dashwood, Lady Brunner, Lady Heneage, Miss Cecil Heneage, Lord and Lady Belhaven, Sir Seymour and Lady Bland, Mr. and Mrs. Charles Wightman, Mrs. Herbert Norman, General Sir Martin Dillon, Miss Monkhouse, Mr. E. R. Broadbent, Dowager Lady Blois, Misses Blois, Mr. and Mrs. Guy Gold, Mr. Ridley Smith, Miss Smith, Sir Robert Hermon Hodge, Mr. and Mrs. Lee Dillon, Mr. and Mrs. Edward Harbord, Mrs. John Brunner, Miss Brnnuer, Master Brunner, Miss Rosalind Gordon Ives, Mr. Victor Gordon Ives, Mr. R. Grant Reynolds, Mr. and Mrs. Robert Ponsonby, Miss Stanton, Miss R. Stanton, Miss Hilda Dillon, Mrs. Taylor, Sir Ernest Tritton, Bart., Mr. and Mrs. Audrey Blight, Mr. Henry Holland, Mr. Ross M`Gillycuddy, Mrs. T. W. Buckley, Mr.and Mrs. Parker Lowe, &c., After the reception, held by Lady Brunner at 9, Ennismore Gardens, S.W., which was very large attended,Mr.and Mrs. Arthur Dillon left for their honeymoon tour in the South of England. The presents numbered 400 and were from a large circle of Lancashire and Cheshire relations and friends of both families.

LATE JURYMAN FINED.

Mr. F. S. M'Lean, who was snmmoned at the St. Helen's County Court on Wednesday to serve on a jury, was fined 40s. for failing to answer to his name when the jury were called upon. He entered the court shortly afterwards but was too late.

HIGHER EDUCATION IN CHESHIRE.

At a meeting of the Higher Education Committee of Cheshire County Council, the Clerk was instructed to arrange for a three years' tenancy of the gymnasium at tbe Y.M.C.A., Wallasey, in connection with the Girls' High School, Wallasey, and that the use of the hall be for the whole school year, at a rental of £lB per annum. Dr. Markel, of Warrington, has been appointed to repre- sent Cheshire County Council on the governing body of kVarrington Grammar School, and Messrs. G. B. Baker-Wilbraham and .1. Royds and Dr. Bennett on the governing body of Sandbach Grammar School. The following instalments of grants have been made to the following secondary schools : Wallasey Grammar School, 22.60 • Calday Grange Grammar School, £123 ; Crewe Secondary School, £230; Hyde Secondary School, £jo; and Macclesfield Girls' High School, £llO. The Education Committee have approved and promised financial support of the proposal of a new secondary school at Hyde, and have expressed their opinion that a secondary school at Sale for girls should be recog- nised by the county authority, aud that such school should be transferred to the County Council if satisfactory terms can be arranged. The committee have received an important letter from the Board of Education with regard to schemes for county secondary schools. Mr. W. N. Brae writes :—" lam to explain that the Board make schemes for the regulation of individual schools under certain statutory powers,which are only exercisable when the school has, in the form of buildings or otherwise, some definite endowment, sub- ject to a charitable trust. They would not be prepared to draw up schemes for schools of other kinds and to affix their seal thereto, as the adoption of that course might give the impression that the schemes had, or were intended to have, some force and authority which they would not really possess. The Director of Education said there was a school at Pickmere (in the Lymm district) with only nine chil- dren on the registrar. It was the smallest school in Cheshire.—Dr. Hewitt: How far is the nearest school ? Mr. Ward : Not mire than two miles. It was decided that the ss.holars should be drafted to other schools.

A Daring Fox

The meet of the South Cheshire hounds on Tuesday was at Combermere Abbey. Among others bunting were Lord Puwis, Lady krtimr Grosvenor, General and Lady Pole-Carew, Lord Kenyon, Mrs. Corbet, Mr. Algernon Stanley, Lord- and Lady Crichton, Lord Gerald Grosvenor, and Baron Schroder. A fox which jumped up in the Island cover almost immediately. went to ground. Hounds afterwards drew blank that once famous cover - Wrenbury 3losses. While the hounds were breaking up a fox which was chopped in the park below the keeper's lodge, another fox which fled from the Rhododendrons picked up a chicken by the stables, and, despite a bus-and-cry raised by the stable boys, ,carried it off to the woods. A. racing burst of twelve minutes' duration from the Bridge cover, over Combermere Park, to ground at Burley Dam partially redeemed a disappointing day.

ACCIDENT. \

Mr. .1, G. Mullins, President of the Earlestowu Co-operative Society, met with a nasty accident last Thursday morning while at his work in the Viacluet- Works. Coming down a Indd,?r, his foot slipped and he fell on his back. The back of his head was badly cut, and bled crofusely. After a time he walked home with assistance; and Dr. Watkins stitched up the wound which appears so far to be going on well.
