TIT E EXAMINER.

JANUARY 12th 1907.

We are not sure that the THE LABOURER latest piece of Canadian AND THE enterprise can be looked LAND. upon with entire approval, though it is certainly very spirited from the Canadian point of view. Our kin beyond the sea, it appears, are sending over agricultural missionaries to give advice and instruc- tion to labourers who are minded to emigrate to our vast American Colony. They are farmers and landowners who have themselves been agricultural labourers, and they are to visit the various farming centres and talk with would-be emigrants about the advantages of Canadian farming. This would be excellent if our agricultural dis- tricts were suffering from over-population, but the contrary of this is notoriously the case. Some official figures published the other day showed at what an increasing rate land in this country is going out of cultivation. Numerous reasons might be assigned for this tendency, all of them probably having some soundness in them ; but one of the soundest of reasons is that the farmer can no longer obtain the requisite labour just when he needs it. Under these circumstances we do not feel called upon to applaud, without consideration, a new effort to divorce the labour from the land, even though it be made in the interests of Canada. Exceedingly vain° ideas are entertained rather generally, we are afraid, as to the character of the benfits derivable from emigration. That it can never be beneficial to a country to remove from it its best bone and sinew is a truth that is not at all widely understood. The agricultural missionaries from :Canada are expected to make many converts. " The work of recruiting," it is remarked, "will last about three months, in which time, it is hoped, the expert farmers will have succeeded in tapping every agricultural district in the kingdom." Hen will be induced, that is to say, to leave the places where they are most wanted. The agricultural labour that has drifted to the towns will be left there. What will take place is a further denudation of the villages. If this were to be carried on to any great extent it would be a patriotic duty to take steps intended to induce the agricultural labourer to remain at home, whereas now we are expected to applaud, from patriotic and imperialist motives, the itinerant Canadian farmers who have come over to entice him away. The situation has a certain piquancy. It will also have its particular serviceableness if it directs attention to the real nature of the emigration problem.

There cannot be a reasonable doubt that we need farm labourers almost as much as Canada. If we are to keep them, notwithstanding the allur ing prospects that are held out by the Canadian missionaries, we must take care that their life shall be worth living. That will, in future,become an absolute condition. With the towns calling to him, with farmers who have been agricultural labourers telling him of the potential riches of the great West, the labourer will not remain in the neighbourhood of the farm just for the farmer's convenience. There must be something substantial to keep him there. The best thing would be a bit of land, and the least induce- ment should be a healthy and comfortable cottage. It has not yet been found practic. able to build reasonably good cottages for agri- cultural labourers at a cost of less than 2250. This means a rent of 2s. 6d, a week, at least, which is more than it is possible to pay. Lord CARRINGTON, as he told us some time ago, finds his remuneration not in the rent of the cottage but in the rent of the farm. The cottages let at is. a week, which means at a loss of about is. 6d. a week on each ; but farms let more readily and at better prices because there is always a certaiuty of extra labour within easy reach. If landlords generally had been foreseeing enough to put in operation some such plan as that of Earl CARRING- TON, we might have seen the Canadian farmers I recruiting in the towns instead of in the country.

The death of the Queen of

THE QUEEN OF Hanover is a reminder that HANOVER'S DEATH. the first signature attached to the Act of Allegiance presented to Queen Victoria at Kensington Palace was that of her eldest surviving uncle, Ernest Duke of Cumberland. The connection between the two countries ceased for the simple reason that the crown of Hanover was limited to the male line, and it passed on the death of William IV.—the Hanoverian Kings of England having been also Sovereigns of Hanover— to his eldest surviving brother. The Duke of Cumberland took his leave of the English Court —to nobody's very great regret as it happened— and became the new Hanoverian King. There was even greater cause for congratulation his

departure after 1866. It would have been an unhappy business if England had been involved in Hanover's fortunes when the great war began between Prussia and Austria. Ernest Duke of Cumberland had been succeeded in Novem- ber, 1851, by his son, and George V. of Hanover, grandson of Gaorge 111. of Euglind was the husband of the Queen who, on—the eve of her ninetieth year, has just passed away at Gmiinden in Austria. King G-aorge, who was born to sorrow as the sparks fly upwards—he had but one eye, and that he lost through an. accident —lost his kingdom also. He threw in his lot with Austria, and that politically speaking, was the end of him. He did not know, any more than others, the scheme of consolidation on which Bismarck had entered. The Hanoverian army inflicted on Prussia the one signal reverse during the war, but that was not enough. Weight of numbers told. Bismarck's power was made manifest, and from that time the King and Queen were exiles frora Hanover.

The prominence to which Tun FUTURE OP labour attained at the last Laßonn. General E'ection was

followed in Warrington by an attempt to re-organize the labour forces of the town, but unfortunately, up to the present, the efforts in this direction have not • met with success. It was recognized that the exist- ence of three bodies—The Trades Council, the Labour Representation Committee and the Right to Work Committee—involved an over- lapping of effort and an unnecessary wastage of funds in an endeavour to achieve one common ohject and the proposal was made that they should amalgamate. With, we think, a more enlightened and broader view of matters the L.R.C. suggested that any new authority which was formed to replace the existing bodies, should include in its ranks trade unionists and all reform societies independent of the political parties. The trade unions of the town have, however, by a majority of 16 votes to six decided to maintain an attitude of isolation and have resolved that, rather than co operate with reform societies and socialists, they will revise the rules of tha Trades Council so as to permit of that body taking political action. Apparently they have temporarily lost sight. of the motto which they advance So frequently ." Union is strength " and, with Councilloc Stott, we are inclined to think that very little lasting benefit to the cause of Labour will result from trade union action alone. It is to be hoped that the present failure to unite the labour forces will not I rove final, and that ultimately a moie satisfactory I settlement wilt be arrived at.

There is a busy time ahead LIBERAL ACTIVITY, for local politicians, and evidence vi I shortly be afforded of the renewed activity of Liberals in this constituency and in the adjoining iivision of Knutsford. As we had the pleasure of announcing last week, Warrington will on February Ist be honoured with a visit from the Right Hon. Sydney Buxton, M.P., Postmaster-General, who will address a meeting arranged by the Junior Liberal Association in the Parr Hall. It is a long time since Warrington had the opportunity of welcoming a Liberal Cabinet Minister and the meeting promises to rank worthily with the many magnificent demonstrations held by Local Liberals of late years. Mr. B urton is a most capable and hard-working public servant and during the short time he has clntrolled our national postal system he has effected some valuable reforms which will be appreciated by the , workers in that department. Though the War- rington meeting will be an event of outstanding political importance, the Liberals of Stockton , Heath are not to be outdone in the zeal for prop ' aganda work. On the evening preceding Mr. Buxton's visit (January 31st) a meeting will be held in the Hall Cafd, Stockton Heath, and the speakers will include Mr. Henry Vivian, M.P. and the member for the division (Mr. Alfred J. King.) The great revival and expansion of Liberalism in this part of a large constituency helped in DO small measure to secure Mr. King's return. There must be no relaxing of efforts to keep progressive ideals to the fore and as the forthcoming meeting is certain to be highly interesting we anticipate a large muster.

A most boring and pro- - " ROT." traced Council meeting on Tuesday evening was enlivened by one incident which was dramatic while it lasted. The Meyer was reading a report from the electrical engineer about an accident at the lighting station which might have had serious consequences. The report was garnished with considerable detail and the Mayor made the most of the heroism of Mr. Mathias and his•staff in venturing into a room full of steam and live electricty. All at once Councillor Wilson jumped to his feet and in a voice which trembled with horror and righteous indignation said: "A Councillor here is calling out rot." (Sensation.) Then the Mayor, in a voice equally tense said "If you name the gentleman he must apologise to the chair." (More sensa- tion.) The accuser pointed a finger at Councillor Cunnington and " named " him. 'Orford's massive representative refused to apolog:se. The Mayor: You won't ?—Councillor Cunnington : N 9! (still more sensation). [Things looked like coming to an impasse when Councillor Cunnington denied using the word '"rot," and the Mayor accepted his denial. Everybody at once breathed again, and the humdrum of ordinary business proceeded. The incident raises a point which was not really settled. We hold the opinion that a Councillor has a perfect right to say "rot " sotto voce as often as he likes. He might even say it out aloud without committing a very great breach of procedure. Of course it would be rude, but we are accustomed to even ruder things in the Warrington Town Council. So long as the remark was not audible to the speaker no offence was committed and Councillor Wilson was guilty of a piece of unnecessary interference when he drew the Mayor's attention to the utterance of what he conceived to be the word " rot." Altogether there was much ado. about nothing, but while it lasted the incident created quite a sensation.

However up-to-date War- UP-L,-DATE ringtou may he in the SANITATION. management of its sanitaryl works at Longford,there are other aspects of the public health question in which th 3 town is miserably behind-hand. The worst system in vogue locally is that for the disposal of night-soil. Instead of beicg sent out in the small hours these noisome vehicles aro to be met in some districts quite early in the night poisoning the air and the unlucky•pedestrian at the same time. How much longer such relics of barbarism are to be tolerated we know not, but in any case the work might be carried out on less objectionable lines. Our sewerage system is I congested, and a legacy from the past. We cannot blame the Sanitary Committee of to-day, for they may well hesitate to enter upon a renewal scheme which would cost £lOO,OOO at least. But there are things which they have left undone, all the same. For instance, sewer ventilation in -War rington is obtained through the ordinary man-holes or street grids. Obviously it is inefficient for not long ago there was the distressing fatality caused by an accumulation of gases in one of the sewers. i Besides being inefficient it s unhealthy. Up-to- date municipalities are constructing tall shafts which carry the sewer gases high above the streets and dissipate them in the open air. In some towns—even in Manchester—the hollow tram- way standards have been connected with the sewers [and used as ventilating shafts. We believe this idea was suggested to the Warrington authorities long ago, but it has not been utilised. Manchester is tackling the question seriously and a suitable area is to be shortly ventilated by means of the tram- poles. Is it not reasonable to suppose that we should never have had the Howley fatality if some such simple and efficient means of ventilation had been adopted? The idea is thoroughly practic- able and we hope the health authorities of the town will show themselves to be alive to the necessities and improvements of the day.

Of all social organisations AN Aarrr-Surerne the Salvaticon Army is BURE certainly the one which gets down quickest to the bed rock of the needs of the in ISieg. Is scope and uplifting pewer are immense and moreover it is never " weary in well-doing." Tee latest Army venture is unique, and charactsristic of General Booth's unflinching resolve to feel a remedy wherever he locates a social disease. Until the dawn of the present year an " ti-Suicide Bureau " had never been heard of,, but thanks to "the General" and his capable organisers it already exists-and has begun wcrk. How neces- sary something of the kind is can be proved by a glance at the suicide statistics of this and other countries. The number of people who commit the rash act of self-destruction is increas- ing alarmingly, and if the " bureau " can do any- thing to check the evil it will be another feather in the cap of the S Ovation Army. We are hope- ful that much will be possible. The sad story of the coroner's inquests too often reveals the fact that the suicide might have been deterred from his insane resolve had there been a helping hand or a friendly voice available when hope had fled. The "Anti-Suicide Bureau " invites the attention of all those who are bordering on the mad con- viction that " there is only one way out of it." G )od advice,, moral and even material help are promised. but no financial assistance. If the scheme has its weak point it is this latter. Maney, we are told, is the root of all evil, and certainly it is often the want of it or the misuse of it which drives some poor creature to a cowardly despair. If one or two millionaires would take up the" Anti- Suicide Bureau "instead of building libraries and endowing colleges they might be enabled to save valuable lives and earn the •indyiug gratitude of thousands of people. Financial assistance sensibly offered would turn many a foot from the grave, ,and we do not see what better use the teeming gold of the millionaire and multi-millionaire could he put to. Whether funds will ever be forthcom- ing -for such a purpose or not, the Salvation Army's new venture ought to be crowned with success. The Bureau is already at work in Lendon, and a branch Ilan been opened in Win- chester. Two vast centres of industrial depression are thus tapped immediately, and in Lendoe a constant stream of " hepeless " callers has drifted in. There seems no doubt that the means of providing the "saving " grace in many a life nas been found. God speed to it!

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Heard in a Manchester tramcar on Saturday night : Lady: Do you stop at the Midland Hotel, guard? Guard : No, mum—it's too expensive. o o

The Hull child who wrote in his essay that alcohol " stingeth like a hatter and biteth like a servant " will probably keep the school-room humour record for 1907. * * *

"If you have tears prepare to shed them now! ' The Vicar of St. Mary gagdalene, Paedington, boasted in 1901 that " many a little Dissenter has been taught the Catholic faith" in his school. *There will be no more conversions, for the heartless London County Council has condemned the school as unsuitable. * *

Sir H. Burdett in his " Hospitals and Charities " points out that it costs six of the leading Church sod- ties ;£16,000 more to raise a sum of ;M20,000 for foreign missions than it does seven of the Noncon- formist societies to raise £777,000—i,e., that the Church slcieties spend 12 per cent, on management, while the non-Church bodies do their work for 8 per cent. •

A certain Warwickshire Vicar is an economist of the strictest type, judging from a circular he sent out appealing for funds for the renovation of the church, and which had the following postcript :—" If you are unable to send us a subscription of any kind, will you, kindly forward us a postage stamp, so that the fund may net lose the cost of addressing to you this appeal." * *

Though the electrical engineer, Mr. Mathias,did not get his advance of salary on Tuesday he will probably succeed at the nest Council meeting. Most of the objections were raised to his having an extra S:5O per year and two premium pupils in addition. Without the latter he is pretty certain to get the " rise" which we believe he has earned by an efficient management of the electrical undertaking. o c,

Some interest will be taken locally in the conviction of a Darwen publican for holding a football gwsep- stake on licensed premises. Sweepstakes have a great hold on the Warrington public and there is more than one licensed house in the tawn which renders itself liable tc a prosecution under the Lottery Act. The penalty at Darwen was ;£5 and costs in one case and the costs in seven other cases. The Chairman of the bench said they were advised that these sweepstakes were illegal whether held on licensed premises, in mills, or in other places. * * *

Counctllor Stott, J.P, is ever anxious to serve the class which he represents on the Council and his elevation to the Magisteral Bench is viewed with distinct pleasure in local labour circles. On Tuesday night the Trades and Labour Council, of which Mr. Stott is a prominent member, heartily congratulated him upon his new appointment. In replying to the vote the new Justices of the Peace made an amusing slip of the tongue. He said he only consented to his name being placed on the Commission because he hoped that in that capacity he .could be of some service to the members of the Trades Council and the working- man of the town. The slip was unnoticed and the sentiment heartily applauded. * * *

The brick-buying extraordinary to which we referred last week did not receive much enlightenment at Tuesday's Council meeting, It seems that the Sani- tary Works Committee twice overlooked Messrs. Richardson's tender, and finally discovered that it was the lowest. Messrs. Richardson will probably believe in the old adage " Better late than never," for the Council decided that the contract should be given to them, thongh the tender of the Warrington Slate Co. Ltd., had already been accepted by the Com- mittee. According to Councillor Pemberton the Corporation will now get the best bricks at the lowest price, which should be satisfactory to the ratepayers. * * *

The Council did well to refer back the recommenda- tion to erect a public convenience on the site of the old bridewell at the bottom of Buttermarket-street. This is one of the best open spaces and most valuable street sites in the boroue and to use it for the pur- pose suggested would ve been desecration. Of what use would be the efforts of the Beautiful War- rington Society if the Corporation were to plant before the public gaze such abominations to the eye as these conveniences?" They should be under- ground, and the fact that the Sanitary Works Com- mittee is hard up for a site is no excuse for proposing to make such an abominable excrescence on the pave- ment of "Howard Place." John Howard was worthy of a better memorial.

* Anglers had a disappointing time last season— indeed one prominent member of the Warrington Anglers' Association stated at the annual meeting on Wednesday that his catches in association waters last season totalled eight ! The year was one of the worst on record and disciples of Izaak Walton are praying that next season there may be fisherman's weather—which is understood to include rain in abundance. People who are not so enthusiastic in the sport will not join in Elie petition, but will rather hope for plenty of sunshine. Incidentally we may congratulate our local pliers of the rod and line ui on. the increased success of their Association which occupies a very proud position among similar organiza- tions and is exceptionally well managed.

-* * * Although the Junior Liberal Associationis now primarily occupied in arranging for the visit of Mr. Sydney Buxton, ALP., the ordinary educational work will be resumed with increasing interest and enthusiasm on Monday evening. An excellent pro- gramme has been drawn up and reflects infinite credit upon the officials who work so energetically for the Assceiation. Monday's meeting will be unique, for Mr. Edmund Osborne, the first chairman of the Association, is to give a paper on " Why I am a socialist." In Mr. Osborne's hands, the subject is sure to be ably treated and an interesting discussion may be expected, while it is certain that the speakers' -views will come in for considerable criticism,

The following good story—which also illustrated King Edward's tact —is told of the late Shah of Persia: The Shah only adopted knives and folks quite recently When he first visited England a banquet was given to him by King Edward, then Prince of Wales, and asparagus, which the Shah had never heard of, figured on the menu. The Shah nibbled gingerly at a stalk, and then threw it on the carpet. To have let it remain on his plate would have been a breach of Persian etiquette. The Prince, with his usual tact, was equal to the emergency. He picked up stalk after stalk, ate what he wanted, and threw the balance on the carpet behind his chair. A moment later thirty guests were following snit—and not a muscle quivered in the faces of the Royal attendants. * * * It is not often that one hears a judge relate an anecdote to illustrate a legal point, but his Honour Deptuty Judge S'rachan resorted to this pleasant device at the Warrington County Court on Thursday. The discussion waged round the way in which an _accident had happened, and the defending counsel said it was impossible for it to have occurred in the manner described by the plaintiff. The Judge reminded counsel of the story of the man who was put in the stocks for frying fish on a Sunday morning. A lawyer chanced to pass that way and in answer to his inquiries the victim told for what trivial offence he was being punished. " That's impossible " exclaimed the lawyer. "Oh " Feplied the man (5 but they've done it !"

* * The Board of Education is responsible for an order which, in Warrington, will certainly he considered a piece of silly red-tapeism. The title of "People's College "is objected to by the big-wigs in London and the Local Committee is requested to find another name for the old school in Arpley-street. Hundreds of Warringtonians will feel resentful, for the school 'has played a big part in the training of those who are the citizens of to-day, and as the People's College it has an old and honoured name. We hear there is a suggestion to call it Brexvtnall School—after-the first headmaster—but we do not think this would be a popular choice. If a change has to be made, why, not Warrington Central School? The school took the place of the one which formerly stOoi on the site of the present Central Station, so the title would not be Inappropriate. - * * Of all the Acts on the Statute Book, probably few are more productive of litigation than the Workmen's Compensation Act. Cases of the most intricate description occupy the attention of the Courts and there is no end of argument and legal hair-splitting. -His Honour Deputy Judge Strachan, K.C., happened to have two compensation cases to deal with at War- rington on Thnrsday, and as one was taken under the Employers' Liability Act, it gave him the opportunity of saying how glad be was to get "off the quicksands of the Workmen's Compensation Act on to the more solid ground of the Employers' Liability Act." Mr. F. Cuthbert Smith, barrister, while agreeing,no doubt voiced the view of the bar when he said "But the sands have not been very unpleasant for some of us." " No," rejoined his Honour, "they have been golden sands ! "

* * * The plans for the restoration, in some degree, of the port of Chester. remind one that the Chester inn at which Dean Swift used to break his journeys between Dublin and London still exists. It is the Yacht, at the corner of Watergate-street, and is overlooked by hundreds of nilgrims who scour the city in search of places with interesting personal associations. These old Chester resting-places for travellers were very careless of their history —none foresaw what an asset -to posterity the mere names of their guests might prove to be. Perhaps the best story that has been preserved through the centuries is that associated with the building that is now (or was .until recently) a. tradesman's shop in Bridge-street, and was once known as the "Blue Posts." In the last year of Queen Mary's reign, the story goes, Dr. Henry Cole, Dean of St. Paul's, lodged one night at the "Blue Posts." He was on his way to Ireland with inners, which he boasted to the Mayor of Chester "would lash the heretics of Ireland." The landlady, a Mrs. Mottershead, overheard the conversa- tion—in those days landladies were industrious in the collection of gossip,—and, fearing for her brother, who was in Dublin, she abstracted the Dean's com- mission and put in its place a pack of cards, in possession of which the Dean next day continued his journey. It is said that the Lord President of the 'Council, with fine native humour, met the situation with,' Let us have another commission, and we will meanwhile shuffle your cards." Queen Mary died before another commission could be executed. In the supeeeding Protestant reign the landlady was --rewarded with a nsosion for her intervention, and the Dean ended his days in the Fleet Prison. —" Ma,nehesler Guardian."

An alarming accident, which fortunately was not attended with serious results, occurred on Saturday night in Wilderspool Causeway, Warrington. A. horse and trap, containing two young men named William Winstanley and John Skelton, of Stretton, was proceeding along the road near St. James's Church when one of the wheels came in contact with a kerbstone at the foot of one of the electric standards. The trap was overturned and the occupants thrown violently into the roadway. The men were taken into an adjoining house and afterwards conveyed to the Infirmary, where their injuries, which were not of a very serious"nature, were attended to. After a few hours' detention they were taken home. The horse bolted, but was stopped by a tram conductor named Smith a short distance away.

Crystal LIMP OIL in barrels or. , drums delivered free.—Sole agent, J. S. GARLASD, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Honour for Mr. Rymer Young. ; Mr. J. Rymer Young, J.P.,,„F C.S., of Warring ton has been elected President of the Pharmaceutical Society of Great Britain in succession to Mr. R. A.. Robinson, L.C.C. Mr. Young, who is now fifty-six years of age, was a student of Owens College, Man- , cherter, in 1870, and later of the School of Pharmacy of the Pharmaceutical Society. He qualified as a pharmaceutical chemist in 1872, and has for many years practised at Warrington. Since 1893 he has been a member of the Pharrnacetuical Society's Council, and for two and a half years he has been Vice-President of the Society and Chairman of the Education and Examination Committee. The Pharma- , centical Society, it may be explained, is a body incor- porated under Royal Charter sixty-four years ago. By statute it has been eLtrust3d with the task of seeing that all persons who carry on the business of a chemist and druggist, or style themselves as such, shall have a competent knowledge of their calling. The Pharmaceutical Society's examination constitutes a severe test, which enables the public to rely on the scientific knowledge and technical proficiency of qualified chemists. The Pharmaceutical Society has a membership of nearly 6,500 persons, all legally qualified to dispense msdicines and sell poisons. Mr. Rymer Young was awarded s medal by the Royal Humane Society in 1899; and he is a brother of Mr. A. H. Young, F.R C.S., who is Professor of Anatomy at the Victoria University, Manchester, and a member of the General Medical Couticil.

Taxation of Land Values. The sixth fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Branch of the English League for the Taxation of Land Values av cs held on Friday, January 4th in Gregory's Cafe. Mr.• Alfred Hahne in..the chair. In the discussion the point was raised whether the landlord could shift the tax. Besides the great authority of John Stuart Mill, who proved that the landlord cannot shift, tax or rent (laud values) it was pointed out that the land- lord takes now as much as he possibly can get, and that while taxes on products of human labour increase the cost of production by checking the supply a tax on land values cann t check the supply; on the contrary in bringing all the idle land that is now kept by speculation, into the market, it would lower the price of land and rents by competition. In discussing , the power of capital it was proved that far more than ' nine-tenths of what is called " Capital " to-day is not "Capital " at all but some form of monopoly: "Land Value" and that by breaking down this huge monopoly of sweating,power of true capital would be completely gone since everybody would have access to the land and the raw materials of Nature, just as the Canadian Redskins defied the offer of the railway engineer to transport rail way building material form one place to the other at the usual European wages. They could defy tha capitalist, because they had access to the land, and unless they were paid more than they could earn by applying their own labnur to the land, they flatly refused. They were free men and not land slaves ! Seeing the enormous effect of the acting two chapters of the "Story of my Dictator- ship " (" A Christmas Dream") has had, it was unani- mously decided to act four more chapters of the same book on February Ist in the Co-operative Hall.

The Examiner, Saturday, January, 12 1907.

New School for Penketh. The Lancashire Einc'ation Committee are this week advertising for tenders for the erection of a school at Penketh, to accommodate 350 sch)lors.

Ragged School and Mission. On Sunday evening the limelight service at the above place was conducted by Mrs. Moores. The pictures shown were scenes from "The life of Christ," and the lessons derived from them were very good. " Ora pro nobis " illustrated, was sung by Mr. W. Wright. Mr. Frank Owen manipulated the lantern. Each scholar was given a mince pie on leaving. Wycliffe Sunday School Tea Party.

The last item in- the programme of amusements was not recorded in our paragraph last week. This was a humorous dialogue by Masters Harry Griffiths, Walter Jordan, George Molyneaux, and Leslie Wall- ington, entitled "Dr. Diaculum." The sketch was well performed, the get-up of the actors being neat, the speech bold, and the mimicry smart.

Old Blues' Re-Union. The annual re-union in connection with the Blue Coat Brotherly Society was held at the Blue Coat School on Thursday evening and proved highly successful. About seventy ladies and gentlemen were present. The first part of the evening was devoted to music, the artistes being Miss Snelling, Miss N. Armstrong, Messrs. Arthur Griffiths, William Brough- ton, and A. Grounds (humorist). Afterwards dancing was indulged in until midnight, Mr. George Lomas supplying the music. Mr. -John Dobson acted as MC. The committee which had had the arrangements in hand consisted of Messrs. J. Dobson, J. Moss, W. Rutter, and Fred Short (secretary).

Drunken Driver's Offence. William Caldwell, of Warrington, was brought up at Newton-le Willows Police Court, on Thursday, charged with having been drunk while in charge of a horse and float, and also with doing wilful damage to windows, to the extent of Gs., belonging to Frank Monks, of Poulton, near Warrington, on the Bth inst. In consequence of prisoner's condition and driving without a light, his and another vehicle collided, causing a serious accident. Afterwards prisoner went to Monk's house and with a stick smashed six panes of glass. For each offence prisoner was fined 40s. and costs, or a month's hard labour in each case in default.

Shorthand Writers' Association. On Saturday night this Association resumed its weekly meetings after the Christmas vacation, a fair number of memb3ra attending at the Winmarleigh Cafe. Real work in the nature of speed practice was undertaken, and the latter portion of the evening was occupied by the reading, by Mr. C. H. Longshaw, of a Federation paper entitled "Three Great Novels and How to read them." The novels reviewed were "David Cppperfield," John Halifax, Gentleman," and " The Little Minister." The writer mentioned three kinds of readers : the one who " rushed" a book, and arrived at the " married and lived happily ever after" termination ; the reader who criticised the characters in a novel, and compared one with another, and a third and wise reader who not only studied the plot, the characters, and the general make-up of a book, but also the literary style of the novelist. The lecture included an interesting resume of the three novels re- ferred to,and tribute was paid to the respective authors. The paper, which proved to be an enjoyable. one, was , followed by a discussion, and the meeting concluded i wtth a vote of thanks to the reader. Handball. In connection with the Liverpool and District Handball League, a very keen and exciting game was played on Thursday night at Birkenhead Gymnasium, between Warrington Gymnasium and Birkenhead Y.M.C.A. From the commencement the play was of a very interesting nature, Birkenhead opening the scoring with a clever throw, when a few minutes minutes later, Warrington were awarded a penalty for foul play against Woods, and Bibby equalized. Half- time : One goal each. Some remarkable play was witnessed in the second half, Holloway, the Warring- ton Captain, scoring two very fine goals from over the half-way line. Later after some good combination between the forwards, Toppin gave a splendid pass to Regan, who registered the 4th goal in the last few minutes for Warrington. The packing of the Warring- ton backs was safe throughout the game. Warrington came away winners by the narrow margin of four goals to three which well represented the night's play. The next League match will be played in the Berough Gymnasium, Palmyra-square, when Warrington meet Liverpool on Wednesday, the 16h inst. Play to corn mence at 8.30 p.m.

NEGLECTFUL MOTHER SENT TO GAOL. Death OF One Child. INQUEST AND VERDICT.

A seri:ms case of alleged family neglect was brought before the Bench at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday morning,when Mary Glover,l,Navigation-street, was charged with neglecting her five children. Mr. Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., sairi-the husband was not charged because he was a very temperao,respectable,hard-working man. The youngest of the children on January 2n4 was examined by Dr. Peacocke, who ordered its removal to the Work- house Hospital, where it had since died. An inquest would be held that day, and he (Mr. Browne) was instructed to apply for a remand, so that they could have the jury's verdict as to whether neglect pro- duced death, and as to whether they would be justified in bringing a charge of manslaughter against the prisoner. Dr. Peacocke spoke to visiting prisoner's house on January 2nd. He found the youngest child, aged eleven months, suffering from tubercular disease, and con- sequent emaciation, which, in his ?pinion, were greatly aggravated, and the sufferings increased, by neglect. The child weighed eight pounds, whereas the average for a child of the same age was nineteen pounds. The other children were poorly clad, the room they occupied was filthy, and there was practically no bed covering, except a dirty sheet and a few rags. Such conditions were bound to be inju:ious to the health of the children. Inspector rox, N.S.P.C.C., said the case had been under his supervision since February, 1905. He had warned prisoner about her conduct on several occasions, and advised her to be different. She was a very drunken woman, and neglected her home shamefully. She had practically ruined her husband. When they threatened to prosecute her she said "You will never get me there ; I will drown myself first." Witness thereupon got a warrant for her arrest. Prisoner,who said she preferred to be dealt with then, was remanded until the following day. INQUEST ON THE_ CHILD. _ .

The inquest on the deceased child—Arthur Stanley Glover—was held on Tuesday afternoon by Mr. Brighouse. Arthur Glover, wireworks labourer, the father, stated that the child had been weakly since birth,and had been under treatment at the Infirmary since it was two months old. Witness's wife had drunk hgavily, and she neglected her home, the children and himself. He had tried his best to improve her, with the assistanbe of Inspector Cox, but to no avail. Dr. Pea,cocke said he had made a post-mortern examination, along with Dr. Smith, of the child's body. The child was considerably wasted, and, in his opinion, had died from tubercular disease of the mesenteric glands. In answer to the Coroner, he said that was a heredi- tary disease as a rule,but it was p)ssible to be brought on by neglect. The Coroner : Are you prepared to sly this tubercular disease was set up by negle3t on the pat of the parent ? Witness : I eannot say. _ _

The Coroner: This dtild might hava died, even if it had had the best of attention?

I Witness: it is possible. The Coroner observed that it was not a case of man- slaughter. There might be a case of neglect, but he had nothing to do with that. His suggestion was that the jury should return a verdict in accordance with the medical testimony,. and leave it there. The jury adopted this course. THE PRISONER ENTENCED. At the Police Court on Wednesday, Mary Glover, was again charged with neglecting her five children. Mr. A. Browne said the husband was a respectable hard working man, and nothing could be said against him. His wages, averaging 3%. per week, were given to his wife, who, instead of providing food and clothing, spent a large portion of the money in drink. There was prac- tically no furniture, and as quickly as the husband provided bed clothing prisoner pawned the various articles for drink. Oa Thursday last Dr. Peacocke was called in, and a boy aged eleven months, was found to be saffering from tubercular disease, and his condition was so emaciated that he was conveyed to the work- house infirmary, where he'died on Sunday.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for three months.

SEQUEL TO A FATALITY.

Warrington Firm Sued.

I At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Deputy Judge Strachan, K.C., Phcebe Webb, 16, Kent-street, brought an application under the Work- men's Compensation Act, to recover £l5O, compensation for the death of her son, Arthur Wilcox Webb, who was in the employ of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, and who was killed at the Staveley Works,Barrow Hill, near Sheffidld. Mr. F. Cuthbert Smith (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel) appeared for the applicant and Mr.Adshead Elliott (instructed by Mr. J. W. Knowles) represented the respondents' Insurance Company and Mr. F. G. Taylor Aatched the proceedings on behalf of the Pearson and Knowles Co., Mr. Cuthbert Smith said the claim was brought under the Workmen's Compensation Act by Phcebe Webb, who was the mother of a boy named Arthur Wilcox Webb. The boy was in the employ of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles and on August 27th he was working at the Staveley Works in the erection of blast furnaces. He was beating rivets on a scaffold when he fell and sus- tained such severe injuries that he died on the following day. The earnings of the boy during the three years preceding his death, together with allowances, amounted to £B4 us. 3d. The mother, in particulars, alleged that she was wholly dependent upon the earnings. The respondents had filed an answer in which they not only denied that she was wholly dependent on the earnings of her son, but even that she was partially dependent, and set up as a fact that they had paid the funeral expenses of the deceased, which were only recoverable under the Act if no dependents were left. That admis- sion as to the funeral was, he (counsel) held, an admis- sion of liability.

A lengthy legal argument ensued between his Efouour and Mr. Smith, the latter contending that the ‘sum of £4O which had been paid into Court was wholly inadequate. The mother, he said, was a widow and for the last six years had been unable to earn anything as she was a sufferer from chronic rheumatism and had been bedfast.

he had a married -datighter,a Mrs. Miller whose husband only earned from 255. to 27g. per week and was in indifferent health. Mrs. Miller allowed her mother and the boy to live in the house and she took as payment whatever wages the boy sent home. Applicant stated that the boy would have been out of his time if he had liyed to finish the work at Stavelely. The money she received from him, 155., 10s. ors. at a time, was always, given to Mrs. Miller. By Mr. Elliott : She had lived apart from her husband and he was the father of her deceased son. The birth was registered in the usual way and she did-not leave a blank in the column in which the name of the father of the child ought to have been placed. His Honour objected to the question and said the respondents admitted their liability to pay some com- pensation. How could the paternity of the chill offset this case? _ _ _

Mr. Elliott : It is an illegitimate child. His Honour: I don't think this is at all a-creditable defence, and it is not a defence I shall assist in any way. It is an attempt to hurt the feelings of the woman who is making this application. N 3 notice whatever has been given tint such a defence was going to be replied upon, and I think you are precluded from raising it in any event by your adtnission of liability. Witness had said her husband was the father of the child. _ _ _

Applicant, in reply to further questions, said she had a son who was working, but he had sent no money for her support. She had been wholly dependent on the earnings of the deceased boy. She had another son who worked as a boiler maker, bathe had not given her any financial assistance for three or four years. Mrs. Charlotte Miller said the deceased and her mother lived with her. The boy used to give his mother the money he earned and she gave it to witness. She (witness) never knew that her mother received any money except froth her son. Mr. Elliott: Do you suggest that a weekly wage of about ss. was sufficient to keep the boy and his mother? —lt has kept them. Mr. Elliott for the respondents said applicant's case' was based on the supposition that the mother was wholly dependent on the boy's earnings and that without his support she had no means of subsistence. The Judge: lam satisfied that she was not wholly dependent, but that she was partially dependent on the •boy's earnings there is some evidence. Mr. Elliott said he admitted that there, was evidence that to some extent the mother was partially dependent on the earnings of the lad and the respondentshad given the matter careful consideration and come to the con- clusion that if they paid into Court £4O that sum would under the circumstances of the case, be ample to reward her for any pecuniary loss she had sustained. When the boy was working at home his wages very rarely indeed came to as much as 6s. a week. Out of that sum he had to be kept and there was very little remaining for the mother's maintenance. She was therefore only dependent on him to a very small extent. His Honour said he could not ignore the fact that the boy was approaching the end of his apprenticeship, and there was a reasonable expectation that he would con- tinue to share the increased wages with the mother. He did not think ss, a week represented that reasonable expectation, and he awarded the applicant £6O with costs on scale C and counsel's advisory fee. Mr. F. G. Taylor, on behalf of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, said the defence in the action had been in the conduct of an insurance company, and the line of defence on which his Honour commented was nottunder the control of the Pearson an' Knowles Company in anyway. _ _

His Honour : lam very glad to hear that. Mr. Taylor: The company employ 2,0)0 workpeople, and so amicable are the relationships between the masters and the men that every accident happening in the works is settled by the men's committee themselves.

ACCIDENT AT MESSRS. CREFIELD'S.

Results in County Court Action.

At the Warrington County Court on Thtirsday, before his Honour Deputy Judge Btrachan, K.C..'Harry Carter brought an action under the Employers' Liability Act againstAJessrs.Joseph Crosfield and Sons Ltd.,to recover £7B damages in respect of an accident which occurred on September 11th, 19)8, whereby the plaintiff's finger was crushed in a soap stamping machine at which he WAS at work. Mr. F. Cuthbert Smith (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel)appeared for the plaintiff and Mr.Xdshead Elliott (instructed by Messrs. Payne, Galloway and Payne) for the respondents. Mr. Cuthbert Smith said the plaintiff, who was fifteen years of age, was injured by getting his finger crushed in asoap stamping machine. He alleged that the accident happened owing to the negligence of the employers in not discovering or remedying a defect or of someone in their employ whose duty it was to do so. The hand soap-stamping machines were capable of turning out a good deal of soap in the course of a day. To every machine a number of girls were accredited and it was their duty to wrap and pack the soap as it was received from the machine. Consequently, if the work was not done quickly enough the girls were kept waiting. Formerly the stamping was done with one • hand, but as accidents happened orders were given that the boys were to work the machine with both hands and thus keep their fingers away from the die. On the date in question plaintiff was stamping when he noticed that a nut on the machine was loose and called to a man named James Cook, whose duty it was to screw it up. Cook evidently had something else to do and told the plaintiff tq go on working, and he would attend to the machine pr6sently. Girls were waiting for soap to wrap, so the boy continued .werkinr, and the stamp, after going up in the ordinary way, suddenly rebounded and creshed the top- of the first anger of his left hand, causing him very severe pain. He ran screaming to another boy and after the injury had been bound up he was taken to the Infirmary where the top of the finger had to be removed. Mr. Steel, who was on extremely good terms with Messrs. Crosfield, was invited to inspect the machine whilst at work and did sa along with a representative of the Insurance Company. When a die which was stated to be the one in use ,when the accident happened, was placed in the machine, exactly the same thing occurred the rpper die rebounding and striking the lower part of the machine. Plaintiff was called and said the machine had often not acted properly and his fingers had been just touched once or twice before. He corroborated Mr. Smith's statement as to how the accident occurred and was emphatio that he used both hands to work the mairlaine. After the accident Alfred Long, the foreman of the department, said "I'll bet you were stamping with one hand" but witness said "I was not" and another employee coroborated him. William Warburton, another boy employed in the department, said he heard plaintiff call to Cook but he did not think Cook went to the machine.

John Wright, aged fifteen, who was working near, said that in his opinion the plaintiff was using both lands, although witness did not actually see the accident. Elwin Tatlock, soap stamper, who said he met with an accident thirteen months ago whilst working a machine with one hand, said that when the nut was loose the die came down more easily. Mr. T. Steel, solicitor, then related what took place when he visited the works to inspect the machine. Three dies were used in succession but it was admitted that not one of \ them was the die actually worked on the machine when the accident occurred. Witness insisted that the die in question should be pro- duced and the man in charge then fixed another which he definitely stated was the one used when. plaintiff was injured. When he began to work the machine two or three pieces of spsp stuck in the die and one was crushed. The most remarkable thing, however, was that on two occasions the die came down to the lower part of the machine, as it was alleged it did on the day of the accident. When they left the works witness remarked to the representative of the Insurance Company" In face of that, are yon not prepared to settle ?' but the representative said he was not. His Honour, at the invitation of Mr. Elliott, decided to visit the works_to see the machine at work.

TERRIBLE WORKS FATALITIES.

INQUESTS LAST NlGtir. -- Young Mil Killed at Messrs. Fletcher,

Russell's.

Two terrible works fatalities were inquired into by Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, and a jury at the the Warrington Police Station last evening., The first case was that of Johnson Gill Baxter, aged twenty-three years, who resided at 17, Southern-street, Stockton Heath, and how was killed at the works of Messrs,. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., on Thursday morning. He was oiling the bearings of a shaft when by some means he got entangled and was whirled round, his body being terribly mutilated. He lived for half an hour.— At the inquest Mr. A. Lewis, Factory Inspector, was present for the Home Office ' • Mr. W. Forshaw appeared on behalf of the firm; Mr. E. Draper represented the relatives, and Mr. A. Browne attended in the interests of an insurance company. George Gill Barter, joiner's apprentice, 17, Southern- street, Stockton Heath, said deceased, who was his brother, was employed as an oiler and greaser at the works of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company Limited. Witness last saw him alive at 5.30 on Thurs- day morning when he was in his usual health. Deceased had been employed by the firm ever since the opening of the Palatine Works about eight years ago. William Henry Gamble, grinder employed by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company Limited, said it was deceased's business to oil the bearings of the shaft which was working about ten feet from the ground. To get to the bearings he had to use a laddLr which he placed against the wall. Witness heard banging and knew that something must be wrong. On running to the shaft he saw a mass of something going round it. The engine was stopped instantly and deceased's body fell from the shaft.

In answer to Mr. Forshaw, witness said the deceased was a good and careful workman. Mr. Lewis said that had the deceased been called upon to mount a ladder resting against the shaft itself, the firm should have provided a guard on the top of the ladder between him and the shafting. The information given to him at the works, however, was that the ladder rested against a brick pillar and deceased reached over to oil the shaft.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." Mr. Forshaw said the directors of the firm very much regretted this sad accident. It was the first which ha'd occurred at the Warrington works ever since they had been in existence—extending over a period of twenty- five years. The firm provided every care and attention for the protection of the men against accidents, but sometimes the unexpected happened, and that had been so in this case. The deceased was a good and careful workman and the directors desired him (Ur. Forshaw) to express their sympathy with , the relatives in th,,ir sad bereavement. •

Mr. Draper thanked th 3 directors for the expression of sympathy and trusted that later it would take a practical form. The Coroner: That is what I always say. Thera are very sad circumstances surrounding this accident. Theleceased had na father or mother and was practi- cally the worker for the family. By getting this position he could keep all those who were dependent. Mr. Browne: I represent the insurance company and those circumstances will be taken into consideration.

Fatal Overtim3.

Boy Killed at Messrs. Itylidb. Pag,

The second inquiry concerned the death of Philip Hansbury, aged fourteen, 17, Naylor-street, who met his death whilst working at the factory of Messrs. Ryla,nds' Bros. Ltd., on Wednesday eveni&r. Mr. W. Peter Rvlands attended on behalf of the firm. 'Thomas Hansbury, bricklayer, 17, Naylor-street, father of the deceased, said the boy worked' as a wire winder at Messrs. Rylands's and witness last saw him alive at 6.10 on Wednesday morning when he went to work.

As the accident ocnurred after seven o'clock in the evening the Coroner asked Hansbury as to the hours worked by decelasod.

Witnes; said he had been recently working over-time five days in the week—making the hours on these occasions from six o'clock in the morning to nine o'clock in the evening. The Coroner; I thought it was eight hours' work, eight hours' play, and eight shilling's a day. John Gilfoyle, machine man employed by Messrs. Rylands Bros., said deceased was a bobbin winder. The accident occurred at 7.40 on Wednesday evening. Deceased was directing a strand of wire into a machine when, it was presumed, by some means the strand got caught in the revolving machine with the result that deceased was entangled and killed. Deceased might have vvrappel the strand round his bodyand thus caused the accident. Witness was not responsible for his working over-time. Horatio Slack, foreman, said his theory of the acci- dent was that the boy put the strand of wire round his waist to keep it taut, Instead of holding it in his hand, and got too near the machine. The result was that he got fast in the bracket and was killed. Mr. Lewis: Could a guard be placed round this particular machine to protect it?

Witness: It ‘vonlehe detriniontal to the working of the machine.

Witness admitted that on this occasion he was responsible for the boy having to work fifteen hours. Mr. Rylands : If deceased had held the wire on the right hand side, quite loose. instead of putting it round his body, would there have been any danger of the accident happening? Witness : I don't think so.

Mr. Hylands said. they had had two fatal accidents at th 3 works and both had., been the results of machines of that type. He admitted they were dangerous machines. Thdy must be when they wentspinning round at about 1,000 revoth- tions. Both the fatalities—which were the only acci- dents that had occurred at the machines—wonld, in his judgment, have happened if there had been prac- tically any amount of reasonable protection, because on both occasions it was a case of being drawn into the machine through something catching it. If they put a guard along the machine it would obviously be a di "knit thing for a man to put in the heavy weights of wire and there would be a danger of his dropping the wire and hurting himself. If they had anything but absolute protection there would be nothing to prevent the possibility of an accident.

Mr. Lewis iaid he quite accepted the position raised by Mr. Ryl Inds. They would like to be able to over- come the di:ficulties, but at present they could not. A verdict of Accidental death " was returned. Mr. Rylands said he could hardly say how deeply he felt the accident as it was the first he had experienced since he had bean responsible for the management. He hoped the father would accept his strong expression of sympathy and that later he would come to see him and he would allow him the HI compensation which the law provided. With regard to the matter of over- time, that was hardly germane to the inquiry, but he was much shocked to hear the lad had been employed in that way. He understood it was in connection with an urgent order which was bound to be delivered. Fre had no idea a boy of that age would be at work. He had taken steps to stop such overtime in another depart- ment and he hoped in future it would be entirely dis- continued in the works.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

ALLEGED SILVER THEFT. CEIARGE AGAINST WIDNES MEN.

At Widnes on Monday, Joseph Carrol, of Albert-road and Michael Riley, of Esher-street, were charged with stealing and receiving 81b. 10azs. of silver, to the value of £2l, the property of J. N. Dennis and C 0.,. copper smelters, Widnes.—Superintendent Strickland asked for a remand until next Monday, as it was necessary to make a test of the silver.—lt appeirs that on Wednes- day Carrot went to a shop in Liverpool and offered the silver for sale. He was told to call again the next day. He went to Liverpool again, and a member of the Liverpool police force was present, and Carrol was questioned and taken into custody. In consequence of the statement he made Riley was arrested in Widnes on Friday night. Riley said he had not seen Carrol since New Year's Day, and when charged said he knew nothing about it. Carrol said, "I did get it from Riley on Wednesday, and he asked me to sell it for him.' The case vs adjourned until Monday next. Riley was allowed bail—himself in £5O and two sureties of £25 ; and Carrol—himself in £l5 and two sureties of £2O.

WIDNES WORMY'S DSATiI.

A remarkable Widnes character in the person of Mr. Walter Bray, better known as the "old herbalist," aged seventy years, of Church-street, Widnes, has just pissed away. Originally a chemical labourer, who only received a meagre education in his schooldays, he became master of no fewer than five languages—Latin, Greek, French, German and Hebrew. ide also found time to devote to the science of phrenology, which he also 'mastered, while theology found in him a clever exponent. As a politician he was well versed ia the current topics of the day. To show his ele3toral eccentricity, he vowed at the last municipil election that he would not vote for the publican candidate who had charged twopence for a glass of beer. He also found time to dabble in philosophy and botany, and from the latter his familiar title of the "old herbalist" was derived. He mastered shorthand, and became so practised that he taught others. During his later years one often slay him going his rounds selling tea. His personality and goodness of heart endeared him to many, and Widnes will undoubtedly be the loser by his death.

RUNCORN CYCLE CLUB.

On Monday evening meeting of the members of the . Runcorn Cycle Club was held at the Brunner Guild Hall. Mr. R. H. Pringle presided and congratulated , the club upon a very successful year's working. There , was a vary much better balance than last year. The membership was about the same, so it looked as though the expenses had been considerably reduced. He especially congratulated the club upon the success of the cycle parade. It exceeded all their expectations, and they were all very pleased that it turned out so well. The resignation of the treasurer, Mr. W. H. Mack, was announced. The financial report stated that the balance at the beginning of the year was P.,2 153. 3d., and the , receipts-and expenditure left a balance of .£6 14s. lid., I from which £3 will be taken for medals. The reports I were adopted. The following elections took place : , President, Mr. S. Taylor; vice-presidents, the Right I Hon. Sir John Brunner, Alderman N. R. Norman, and Messrs. F. J. Norman, H. Congrieve, and R. H. Posnett ; secretary, Mr. G. A. Kirkham ; treasurer, Mr. G. Powell ; captain for Wednesday section, Mr. Borman ; sob-captain, Mr.G.H. Messrs. Hindley, Presoott,Stanway, Hedgecock,Ashbrooke,Speed, Booth, Smith, mid Jacksoji. The attendance winners of gold medals were announced as follows :—Saturday section, Messrs. J, Green, \V. I Ashbrook,and T. Tattersall ; Wednesday section,Messrs. J. Stanway, G. W. Wheat, and R. Burnam. The Chair- -1 man's suggestion of the club combining with the May Queen festival was considered, and while it was stated that if the festival wore made a town affair the club would support it, the meeting resolved to await the decision of the general meeting of the festival before committing themselves to a decision. It was decided to wind up the season with a dinner, concert and dance.

WIDNES TOWN COUNCIL. FREE SPEECH QUESTION REVIVED.

At the Widnes Town Council meting, held in the Town Hall on Tuesday, over which his Worship the Mayor (Alderman Samuel Ovens) presided, the terms of the settlement of the fre:, speech controversy came on the tapis for the approval of the Council, together with a resolution thanking the ex-Mayor for the efforts he had made in bringing about a settlement. Mr. Clarke moved, "That the Council thank the ex-Mwr (Alderman Quinn) for his endeavours to bring about a settlement of the question, and express their regret that owing to the defiant attitude of the Salva- tion Army his, efforts to vindicate the law, and at the same time settle the dispute in a law-abidiug and peace- ful manner, were unsuccessful." Mr. E. Wood sconded. _ _

i\fr; 15. 'Lewis, who championed the cause of the Salvation Army throughout the controversy, repudiated the resolution, declaring it to be maladroit, and having the intention other than of thanking the ex-mayor.

Aidertnan Sutton Timmis moved-as a furthe; amend- ment that the words" owing to the defiant attitude of the Salvation Army" be struck out.

Mr. Butlough seconded. After a long discussion it was carried. the Council agreeing to acc3pt the 9,:nended resolution of Mr. Clarke.

Mr. Lewis, in moving the adoption of the Health Committee's minutes, stated that the death rate for the part month was 13'Gras against 22.2 last year,a remarkably low death rate for this time of the year. The town was wonderfully free from infectious disease. The birth- rate was satisfactory. It was also decided to invite the Mayoress (Mrs. Owens) to open the huge gas tank at the gasworks,which is now approaching completion.

RUNCORN RUR kL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there were present :—Mr. George Piling (presiding), Rev. E. R. Nunn, Messrs. C. E. Linaker, T. Wright, T. Baxter, S. Woodwarc,, C. E. Linaker, jun., J. Hallows, C. Roylance, J. Whitley, J. Spruce, H. Eyes, A. Broome, H. Mawdsley, J. Whitlow, J. H. Davies, H. M. Davies, T. Guest, E. Brotherton, T. Autwis, J. Rutter, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Farrington (inspector), W. Diggle and J. Swinton (sur- veyors), and Dr. Adams (medical officer.) Complaints having been made as to the lighting of London-road past the canal bridge at Stockton Heath, it was decided to place two new lamps on the roadway. —Mr. Whitley, in seconding the proposition, said the question of the lighting of the streets in Stockton Heath and district was hecoming a very serious one, as it was at present very unsatisfactory. In answer to Mr. Woodward Dr. Adams reported that he had received notification of two cases of scarlet fever at Kingsley. He had not however, been able to visit the cases.

111-r. Woodward said he had been informed that the cases were reported over a fortnight ago and nothing had been done by the public health authJ rity. He understood that one child in the peeling stage of scarlet fever had been running about the village. That state of things wanted carefully going into. They were !pending - £4OO a year for the upkeep of an Isolation hospital and for such a state of things as that to arise was a crying scandal. He did not blame any man unheard but it seemed to him that serious enquiry should be mule into the circumstances. It was no •me them spending so much money to prevent the spread of disease if that kind of thing was to be countenanced. _ .

Dr. Adams suggested that the remedy lay iu.prosecut- ing under the Public Health Act. Mr. Woodward: There is another remedy. When a notification takes place a visitation should be made immediately. Dr. Adams: I have arranged to go over to Kingsley. Mr. Pilling: It is too late now. It is eighteen days since notification. Dr. Adams: I will enquire into the cases reported by Mr. Woodward. Mr. Pilling: Is your machine all right? (Laughter.) Mr. Rovlanoe protested that it was not a laughing matter. It was the duty.of the Council to see that their Medical Officer kept in touch with all cases of disease. Dr. Adams reported that during the month there had been 17 cases of scarlet fever and 16 cases of diptheria

MUSICAL APPOINTMENT.

Mr. Samuel Dring, who recently removed to Chester from Earlestown, has been appointed organist at All Saints' Church, Hoole, Chester, and has resigned the position of organist and choirmaster at Winwick Asylum, which he has held for some years, to take up his new duty. ENTERTAINMENT AT WINWICK.

CHARITY CONCERT.

In aid of the Quarter Century Fund of the Church of England Waifs and Strays' Society, a concert was given on Friday evening at the Town Hall. The attendance was small. The Earlestown Male Voice Quartette Party (Messrs. Mather, Turner, Goslin, and Sad- worth), Mr. Ted Pearce (basso), Mr. J. Rabbit (tenor), Miss Carrie Hastings (soprano), Miss Nora Fairclough (violinist), and Miss Airey con- tributed to the programme. The pianoforte accom- paniments were played by Miss Fairclough, of Latchford. Mr. Kelly, of, Liverpool, dalivered short address on the work of the society, and referred to the splendid support they had received from Newton. In the Liverpool Diocese tbe society-bad done a noble work. When he became chairman they had five children boarded out, and an income of £lOO per year. Now they had 200 children, and an income of £4,000. The children were those of the very poor, who were found adrift. After full injuiry they were taken by the society to a home or boarded with foster parents. Each cost £l5 a year to bring up, and for that money they were brought up in a sensible and practical manner. The girls were trained as domestics, and the boys put to trades. One of the girls recently came to the office and gave them £5 17s. of her savings, and an old boy, who is now in America, sent a hundred dollars. Of the cases dealt with, 93 out of every 100 were successful, and there ware no real failures to be recorded. The Quarter-Century Fund was to equip two new homes. .They had got £5OO, and when £l,OOO was raised they would begin to build. They did not want to buy a house, and hoped someone in Newton, perhaps with an empty house, would give them one. No money could be put to a better cause.

ST. HELENS.

SAD ACCIDENT AT ST. HELENS

Mr. Robert Goldthorp Brook, aged sixty-nine, iron- monger, and a prominent Freemason, on Monday night when passing from his shop to the workshop behind, fell into un area about 12ft. deep, fracturing his thigh and sustaining a large wound on the head.

COLLIER'S DEATH FROM HEART DISEASE.

- -- Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held the adjourned inquiry at the Waggon Hotel, Haydock, on Tuesday, into the death of Samuel Plant, collier, who died on Wednesday, December 19. It was thought that death might be due to accidents he had sustained while at work, but a post-mortem examination by four doctors showed that death was-due to heart failure, resulting from the fatty degeneration and dilation of the heart. —A verdict of " Death from natural causes " was returned.

THE POLICEMAN'S LIFE. Thomas Docherty, of Appleton, was at St. Helens on on Monday convicted of an assault on Police-constable Mather. The officer said that in the early hours of Sunday morning he was conveying a prisoner to the Town Hall when Docherty came up and said," You are not going to take him." He struck him a severe blow on.the nose, and made blood fly all over the officer's uniform. He kicked and'fought all the way to the station. Docherty struck the officer even before he spoke to him.—.A fine of 20s. and costs, or a month was imposed.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
WRONG KIND OF NEW LEAF.

Henry M'lntee, a young man, of 42, Water-street, was again remanded at St. Helens, on Tuesday, on a charge of maliciflusly wounding his brother.—The Chief Con- stable said the assault took place on Monday night, the 31st ult. Prisoner had been in the Army, but during the twelve months since his discharge he irld refused to work at all regularly. On this Monday night he quarrelled with his brother because he told him that he would have to turn over a new leaf and start working with the opening of the New Year. They fought and were separated, but APlntee followed his brother out and inflicted two serious wounds on his head with a big stone. Erysipelas had now set in, and the brother had been taken to hospital, so that another remand was necessary.

ST. HELENS OIL STORE ON FIRE. A fire, which consumed most of the stores at a St. Helens oil and hardware shop on Saturday night, caused a good deal of alarm, the flames leaping high in the air and threatening the surrounding property. The fire occurred at 9S, Kirkland-street, where Mr. Thomas Eyres has recently built new premises. Mrs. Byres went into the shed to draw some paraffin oil from the tank for a customer, when it is supposed that a match which she dropped set fire to some straw that had become saturated with paraffin. Immediately the whole place burst into flames, and when Superintendent Lyon and ,the fire brigade arrived, fire was bursting from the shed at all points,and the flames were running up the gable end of a house in Albion-street. Three jets from the main were quickly put on, and the outbreak was con- fined to the shed, which was destroyed. The damage is estimated at about ,glOO, the shed and stock being, fortunately, clverecl by insurance.

* * Although the late Sir Charles Tennant was a man of great commercial activity, he found time to form one of the finest, existing collections of pictures by English masters in his house in Grosvenor-square. Of Sir Joshua' Reynold's works he possessed the famous Viscountess Crosbie, the well-known "Collina (Lady Ger- trude Fitzpatrick)" and the "Fortune Tellers," which came from the Blenheim collection; while his Gainsboroughs included the beautiful canvas, "River Scene -with Barges," and his Romneys "Mrs. Jordan in the Romping Girl." It was Sir Charles Tennant who secured Millais' fine portrait of Mr. Gladstone when the late Duke of Westminster, after the "great betrayal," re- moved the picture from his walls. It was after- wards presented to the National Portrait Gallery. •

At Spelthorne Petty Sessions William Francis Volant Young, 50, tram driver, was sentenced to four months' imprisonment for stealing £283 is. 11d., the money of the Slate and Loan Club, attached to the Fulwell Depot of the London United Tramway Company, of which he was secretary.

The Norfolk Chamber of Agriculture, meet- ing at Lynn, passed a resolution calling upon the county education authority to reduce its expenditure on education. The farmers wanted the boys to commence working on the land at twelve years of age. One speaker said agricul- ture was in such a bad state that if any more burdens were placed upon the industry it would be brought to the ground.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS

The Alleged Poisoning at Padgate. A meeting of the Warrington Beard of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. J. Billinge. There. were also present : Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Patterson, Messrs. W. Hickman, S. Davies, J. Payton, T. H. Ashcroft, G. lhompson, W. Houghton, G. Mather, A. Brooks, A. J. Jolley, W. Ilannagan, W. Brooks, J. Backhouse, R. Bolton, M. W. Seton,J. C. Sutton (clerk) and A. Wright(superintendent of Padgate schools. THE ALLEGED POISONING AT PAD° ATE. The minutes of the Padgate Schools Committee having been read, Mr.iiicK MAN said he hoped there would be a report about the health of the children as it had been stated in the town that many of the children had been suffering from ptomaine poisoning. Perhaps the Chairman of the Committee would be able to assure them that things were satisfactory. Mr. MATHER (chairman of the committee) said he believed some of the children were somewhat indis- posed a week or ten days ago for an hour or two. Whether it was through the Christmas festivities or not he could not tell, but still there was the outbreak of sickness. It was a case of "much ado about nothing "as the children were now all right, and there were no after-effects whatever. Mr. HICKMAN : A great many people in the town have been much concerned about it. Mr. MATHER expressed his surprise at the report published in an evening paper which also issued a huge 'placard. Little things seemed to disturb little minds. Mr. HOUGHTON said people who lived very rear to the institution knew nothing about the outbreak. There was nothing serious. The matter then dropped. THE HOUSE. It was reported on December 28th,that the number of inmates of the House was 466 compared with 521 for the corresponding day last year. NEW LAUNDRY MACHINERY. It will be remembered that the Board recently sanctioned the purchase of a new electric " callendering ' machine to be installed in the laundry. Mr. HICKMAN now asked if the introduction of the machine would affect the electric plant. Had they already sufficient electricity to run the plant without inconveniencing their present arrangements. Mr. THOMPSON said the question would be answered later as the House Committee had the matter under consideration.

DONATION TO THE INFIRMARY. The Local Goverument Board wrote consenting to the Guardians subscribing the sum of £lOO for one year to the funds of the Warrington Infirmary instewl of £lO 10s. the usual subscription. The increased amount will be handed over to the extension fund.

THE N.S.P.C.C. Mr. PARR, a director of the N.S.P.C.C., wrote warmly thanking the Guardians for their donation of fire guineas to the funds of the institution. DEPUTATIONS.

SOME PARTICULARS WANTED. Mr. BOLTON asked if it were true that it took five persons—four members of the Board and the resident medical officer—to go and poichase certain instruments required by the doctor. If so, he considered it out- rageous. The CHAIRMAN said the purchase of the instruments was left to a sub-committee. Mr. BOLTON said that if the facts were as he had stated, it was a complete waste of money. The CHAIRMAN said Mr. Bolton was out of order in raising the matter now. Mr. BOLTON said he would like a return of all the deputations which had taken place of the past year— as to who had constituted them and the cost. He thought it would be an eye-opener. He would place a notice of motion on the agenda for the next meeting.

COMPLIMENTS- OF THE SEASON. At the close of the meeting the CHAIRMAN wished -the Guardians and staff a bright, happy and prosperous New Year.

A Message to the Nation.

As representatives of various Christian bodies in England, we desire to call the attention of out' fellow countrymen to the combined-efforts which are being made to set forth explicity and forcibly the principle of Sunday Observance, We believe it to be literally impossible to exaggerate the importance-of this matter to the well being of the nation.

It is not merely that one day's rest in seven con- tributes vastly to the physical and, mental efficiency of men, women, and children, and tends to make our home life more truly what English home life ought to be. Under the sacred sanction which attaches to the Lord's Day, it is intended that all should have opportunity, in the worship of Almighty God, to escape from the grip of ordinary cares and occupations into regions of higher thought and nobler aspiration. Wel,are convinced that on adequate and reasonable Sunday Observance depends in no small measure the possibility of promoting in England the deeper, the more sacred, and the more enduring interests of our common life. RANDALL CANTAUR : ± FRANCIS, Ar hbishop of Westminister. J. SCOTT LI DGETT, President of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches.

New Year's Day; 1907. We are requested to state that further information as to the Sunday National Observance Movement referred to in the above message, together with full particulars as to the practical steps which it is proposed to adopt, will be supplied upon application to the Hon. Secretaries, 1, Alhemarle-street, London, W.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHiON-HOME.

"GOLDEN VO,ICES.''

The fact that Madame Melba was able to settle upon her son, on his marriage the other day with - Miss _ Ruby,. Otway, the considerable sum of £1,500 a-year, besides bestowing upon himi a castle and an estate neat Killarney, and much elegant furniture therefor, illustrates to some ex- tent what a harvest of gold is yielded by the voice of the world's great prima donna. The earnings of such queens of song as Melba, Patti, Calve, and the rest are rightly—having regard to the. delicacy of the vocal organs and the large sums of vmoney expended in the perfect training of the artist—colossal. Patti has probably broken recent records •in fees. Her highest recorded re- muneration was at Monte Video, where for two seasons of sixty nights she received £1,200 a, night, thus making during a week of three ap- pearances the large Sum of £3,600. Her largest fees at Covent Garden (in the days of Mapleson) were at the rate of £l,OOO a performance.

AMAZON CLOTH "COSTUME.

The neat costume depicted in our illustration is carried out in brown Amazon cloth. The coat fits closely to the figure, the plastron effect Icing arrived at by a line of narrow black and gold braid, which also conceals the join of the little cape-like epaulettes over the shoulders. The waist is encircled with a belt composed of rows i of braid, and the short basque s cut with a square-shaped tab at each side to 'follow the line of the plastron. It is outlined with braid and

adorned with fancy brown buttons. The' sleeves are full at the top, and finish at the elbow with loose hanging cuffs edged with braid. The skirt is plain and full, and is trimmed with a strap of cloth edged with braid, the lower edge being cut with ,rnall tabs to match the basque. The narrow roll collar- might be faced with brown velvet, or with some bright contrasting colour such as cherry-red, the hat being of the same bright hue.
