SOCIETY WOMAN'S MEAN TRICK.

The following story of a lady, well known in society, was told me the other day, says a writer in P.T.0., which is as dishonest as theft. The lady in question came to a well-known shop in Regent-street, and asked to look at lovely and .very expensive coats. Choosing one of the most beautiful, she begged to be allowed to have it on approval, as she never purchased anything without her husband's good taste in the matter. This request was granted her, and at a smart wedding next- day she wore it. The following day she returned to he shop with the ceat,_ tell- ing the head man there how sorry she was not to take it, but her husband did not like ;t at all, and thought it very unbecoMing to her. The answer to her was this (fortunately, the artistic head man - had been to this wedding to sketch the. dresses): "Madame, I am sorry, and I 'do not understand it, as I thought I ha.d never seen you look better in anything than in this coat at Lady A.'s wedding yesterday,"

SCENTS AND SENSE,

Some ingenious person has discovered that certain scents have a remarkable effect upon the senses and proceeds in all seriousness to detail extraordinary results of their use. Those of us, we are told, who wish to train as highly suc- cessful business men and women are advised, to have their handkerchiefs properly saturated with essence of peppermint during business hours. Essence of magnolia moves the user to warlike passions. The scent of the violet produces the spirit of placid devotion, while strangely enough tat of the lily causes obstinacy. '

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

(Errs z SURPRISE.—BoiI six or eight eggs for ten minutes, immerse them in cold water, and remove the shells. Cut off each end and take out the yolks with an apple corer, chop the yolks, mix them with 3oz. or 4oz. of finely. chopped cooked turbot or sole and a few chopped shrimps. Add sufficient sauce to bind it, and the, yolk of a raw- egg, fill the eggs with the prepara- tion, roll them first in breadcrumbs, then in beatAn egg, and again in breaderumbs. fry them in a stewpan of hot lard. Dish them in an up- right position in two straight rows on a thin layer of mashed potato, and pour nicely- flavoured white sauce round them.

IFY DEAR NIECES AND NEPHEWS,

„. Well! what do you think of it? Waking m up on Boxing orning and finding about three feet of snow outside I Wasn't it too delightfully seasonable, so ChristmasSy.? I could hardly believe my eyes, and I can tell you it was not long before I was out, old as I am, my dears. In fact, I, must confess I actually indulged in a game of snowballs. Of course, you would not think of such a thing, or of sliding, and other things—but I don't think I'd trust you, any- way. You know I was young myself once—well, as far as sliding and snowballing goes, that is the bright side of things; but, then, we must think what it means to those who have to work, and are prevented by the downfall on Boxing Day. It's this side of the question that compels me to appeal to all my nieces and nephews and their friends to send me spine nice warm clothing for poor, shivering little ones. I have distributed all that has been sent me, so I shall appreciate any further gifts of a similar kind while this beautiful, yet dread- ful weather lasts. My post to-day is not very heavy, but, eolisidering the season, I did not look for a lot of contributions. For those who have sent—Best thanks!

From UNCLE NEI).

REPLIES TO CONTRIBUTORS

NANCY E. P.—lt was kind of you to send me the story, but I have already received the same from another contributor. In future, a tale of about a quarter the length would be better, as I ,dannot spare ' so much space—still, many thanks.

ERNEST H.—One of your tales, Ernest, was also sent in by another member. Please read reply to Nancy E. P.

MARGARET M. P.—Thank you, Margaret. 1 hope you had a merry Christmas. The card is extremely pretty.

ALICE L.—l have not yet received your kind gift, but expect I shall in a day or two, when I will be able to tell you how 1 like it. It is very kind of you.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l will now tell you about "The Happiest Christmas I ever Spent." I believe the happiest Christmas I ever spent was in the year 1903. I rose up early on Christ- mas ''morning, hoping to enjoy myselt thoroughly. After having my breakfast, I went to see a football match, which I enjoyed very mueh. Then came dinner-time, which we were all eagerly looking for. We soon ate the Christmas puddings and some of the turkey, which we left for the following day. Then came the most enjoyable part of the day for me. I went to the Sunday school tea party. After, we had had our tea, we played numerous funny games. The first game we played was: —" Sticking the Donkey's Tail on Blindfolded." The first time I tried I stuck it on its eye; my brother stuck it on his leg, and I don't think anyone stuck it on in the right place. The boy who won the prize stuck it on the thigh of its hind leg. I only won one prize, which my brother did the work to obtain. I was on my brother's back, and he raced two other boys. We played numerous games, including draughts, ludo, ring the bell, bite apple, and other games. Then we had a bag containing nuts, oranges, and apples, which I did ample justice to while we were haying presents from the Christmas tree. I did not have one, because all the presents were gone when my name was taken out of the bag. We then sung a few Christmas carols, and made our way home after an enjoyable time.

From your loving nephew,

ERNEST HARROLD.

enclose a Christmas greeting card, which I hope you will like. I shall only have two days for my Christmas holidays, but I will write and tell you how I spent them. I hope these few contributions.will please you; sorry I cannot send you some poetry. I see the nieces are sending the most contributions. Wishing a bright and happy Christmas,

A gentleman sent his negro servant on an errand to buy. some fish, who was told to get it fresh: - On arriving at the market, Samba went to a fishmonger's stall, picked, up a fish and commenced to smell it, amid much- laughter from the bystanders. The fishmonger got into a rage and shOuted, "What are you smelling that fish for?" "I am not smelling it," re- plied Sambo calmly. "Then, what are you doing?" agked the fishmonger angrily. "1 asked' him what news from the sea?" "And what did he say?" asked the fishmonger. "He said he did not know, because he had not been there for three weeks," replied Slunbo. Know- ing that the fish was not fresh, Samba walked away.

Dear Uncle Ned,—The reason why I know this joke is because at one of our School exhuina- tions we had this for composition.—E. H.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

On two occasions the General Post Office sent back, as " insufficiently addressed," telegrams to Hall Caine, Isle of Man. Such is fame!

Mr. Haldane, Secretary of State for War, enjoys the reputation of being one of the most amiable men in the House of Commons. Tradi- tion leads us to suppose that it is impossible to make him angry. _

Lord Meath, who, by the way, has long been urging the observance of May 24 as Empire Day, says that there is a difference of four inches in height and fourteen pounds in weight _between the average Eton or Harrow schoolboy and the average youth of the slums at the same age.

Mr. Andrew Carnegie wai asked some time ago whether he thought a penniless boy, without influence, stood as good a chance now in America as in his own early days. His reply was that the boy would probably stand a better chance, as there were so many more openings for enter- prising youths. _ _

When on a yachting cruise, King Edward is absolutely in his element. He chooses only his most intimate friends to accompany-him, and little or no ceremony is observed on board. With his Majesty, Who is the best of sailors, it is a case of early to bed, early to rise, and through- out the twenty-four hours the maxim of the simple life is scrupulously obsPrved. Paderewski. when three years old, used to creep to the piano to listen to music; he had violin lessons at the age of six, but claims that Nature was his first teacher. " She taught me to revere her sounds of forest, field, and brook, her smiles and tear's." He now practises at all • hours of day or night, without any rule but his ow it fancy.

Ras Mekonnen, the famous Abyssinian general, who recently died, was the heir-pre- sumptive to the throne of his country. When King Edward was crowned he came to England as the representative of the Emperor Menelik, and brought his Majesty five lions, two zebras, and twenty-five very fine ivory, tusks, as Corona- tion gifts. Ras Mekonnen wore a very curious official costume, consisting of a white-satin cape, trimmed with golds lace, white silk pantaloons, blue merino socks, tan shoes, and an enormous black fert hat.

With the death of Henry Spencer Chichester. second Baron Templemore, the " Fathership of the House of Lords" paFses to the Earl of Lei- cester. Lord Templemore was four days short of his eighty-fifth birthday. He succeeded to the title in the year that Queen Victoria came to the throne, and 64 years ago he entered the House of Lords.

The Crown Prince of Norway, Prince Olaf, is not yet three years old, but a biography of him has already been written. When his parents were living in Norfolk, King Edward and Queen Alexandra were frequent visitors. The child saw his nurses curtsey, and one day made such a pompous and dignified bow himself that the King burst out laughing. The child began to cry at the insult to his dignity, and it is said that King Edward was reproved by the Queen for showing his, amusement.

Mr. Keir Hardie, the leader of the Labour party in the House of Commons, although self- educated, is a man of great refinement and con- siderable learning. He is an omnivorous reader, an authority on history, and delivers a speech without the sliptest hesitation. He lives the life of an ascetic, and were it not for the fact that he is overwhelmed by a passion to benefit his fellow-workers he would never be seen at St. Stephen's. During the session he resides in London; his holidays he spends in Scotland with his family.

-Mr. Frank DiCksee, R.A. tells an amusing story of an artist's forgetfulness. He met Mr. Andrew C. Gow, R.A., at dinner, and in speak- ing about the latter's "Elijah" suggested there was something unusual about the drawing .of the model's ear. Mr. Gow replied,- L.,at as the ear was completely hidden by hair Mr. Dicksee was mistaken. "I will bet you a shilling," said Mr. Dicksee, "there is something wrong with the convolution of the ear." "Done," said Mr. Gow. Next morning Mr. Dicksee received a pcstcard from his friend—" Owe you a shilling on convolution." Mr. Dicksee is now suspected by his servants of secretly gambling or horse- racing.

' Mr. Charles Santley, the veteran singer, says that one of his most embarrassed moments on the-stage was when he was playing in "The Bo- hemian Girl" with Parepa. At the beginning of the last act Parepa and Santley were singing the duet. Parepa .had just concluded her opening phrase, and Santley was preparing to upbraid her, when, hearing the audience titter, be looked roun I and saw a dog which had trotted on to the stage and was staring him in the face. Horribly indignant, the artiste tried .to drive it away with his foot, but to no purpose. There it remained until the singer had finished; then, rising, it walked unconoernedly to the wings.

Mr. Coningsby Disraeli Can claim' a more illustrious kinship than even that iN-hich con- nects him with Lord Beaconsfield. On the authority of Mr. Lucen Wolf—Unrivalled in the field of Jewish genealogy—Mr. Disraeli by a series of links, of which three are on the spindle side, can trace his descent to Samuel Abarbanel, otherwise known as Don Juan de Sevilla, who was wont to subjoin to his signature the addi- tion "of the posterity of Jesse the Bethle- hemite." The Abarbanels are, therefore, the premier family in Jewry.

Superstitious Spaniards are wondering if his Majesty will ever he so bold as to wear, the "fatal ring," now adorning the statue of the Virgin of Almaden. in the Cathedral of Madrid. It was made, so they say, to the order of Al- fonso's father, and by him presented to Qtieen Mercedes. Within a month she was dead, and his Majesty, with only ttnder thoughts upper- most in his mind, presented the ring to another member of the Royal family. She, too, died suddenly, as also did two more possessors of the fatal gift. -Then the King, defiant of fate, placed the ring on his own finger. Three months after the throne of gpain was vacant; the King was dead.

The name of General Weyler, who was so badly injured in the Madrid outrage, was very familiar to newspaper readers a few years ago. He succeeded Martinez Campos in the Cuban command, and introduced the rigorous measures which ultimately brought about American inter- vention. In Spain he has distinguished himself, on more than one occasion, by the rapid suppres- sion of disorder. INCOME-TAX REFORM.

To this month's Financial Review of Reviews Sir Thomas P. Whittaker contributes an important article on the subject of the proposed new. income tax. The article has special significance because it was Sir Thomas Whittaker's report which the Select Com- mittee on the income-tax adopted. and the Financial Review of Reviews is to be congratulated on obtaining what is to a great extent a supplementary report in the shape of an article showing how the committee's income- tax proposals will , work out in practice. Curiously enough the essay which wins the twenty-guinea prize offered by the Review each month is on" Diseountincr the Death Duties." Other articles discuss in a practical way various phases of invistruent, and although the Review is creating quite a new departure by making a feature of political-fnarcisl di,cus.icns by prcrninent public men, it still maintaics fte excellence of its urtioles on sound investment.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR. Thursday evening. There was a good attendance at the I tea, the number being considerably augmented for the ---- meeting which was subsequently held in the Church. NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Mr. B Few presided. INlusical items were given by Authors of letters intended for publication must in Miss Barnes, Mr. W. Tynan, Miss E. Hankey and Mr.E. all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor Balmford and party. The reports of the secretary and treasurer were presented and showed that the Church is as a guarantee of good faith. in a sound position the year closing with a small The New Magistrates. balance in hand. Brief addresses were given by the Sir,-1 understand there is a persistent rumour in the Revs. J. S. Hughes and J. F. Matthews. town that I, in my capacity as Deputy-Chairman of the Executive Committee of the Liberal Association„ am to some extent responsible for the recent selection of Lew magistrates. Vvithout one word of adverse criticism of the gentle- men (all of whom I believe to be good men and true) will you permit me to say that I had nothing whatever to do with the selection and knew nothing of the matter until tho appointments had been made.—Yours faithfully,

Lodge-lane, Warrington. 11th January, 11)07.

JOHN. WILKINSON.

Beamont School ,Headmastership.—Mr. Roberts' Two Versions. Sir,—l am sorry to have to trouble you again, but. since Mr. Roberts has thought it necessary to call into question my knowledge of English words I must ask you to kindly print again two quotations, the first being copied from Mr. Roberts' letter, as printed in your issue of last Saturday, and the second is a quota- tion, copied from your issue of December 22nd, as being your reporter's idea of what Mr. Roberts said,.at the meeting of the Education Committee, with regard to the chances of our local teachers to obtain promotion, as far as be himself was concerned. Mr. Robers in his letter (I cannot cannot call it a reply), of Saturday last says "Your correspondent, Charles Turner charges me with having stated at the recent meeting of the Education Committee, anent the above matter, that none of these men (the 17 looal applicants) are capable of conducting a school with the requisite, skill as teachers and organi,sers. It is hardly neces- sary for me to deny that I made such a foolish statement, or one that could possibly bear any such construction, &c., &c." At the Committee meeting mentioned, according to our local papers, Mr. Roberts is reported to have said:— "There was, he contended, no fair objection to it, unless it was to be made on educational grounds pure and simple. No one had suggested that the best man had not been selected. If it was admitted that the committee had to the best of their knowledge and judgment, selected the best man, there was no case for the opposition. Ho agreed that there might be half a dozen, a dozen, or even the whole seventeen, if they liked, assistant teachers qualified to manage the sch )ol ; but up to what standard? That was the point. If they were content to accept their present standard of management, all 'right. But they did not say that when they advertised &c., scc." Now Sir, if the original speech, and the denial be read side by side I defy anybody to say that they could possibly be from the same man, if they had not heard previously that that was actually the case. They are simply marvellous words, if both utterancto mean the samelthing or anything near it, and my knowledge of English, must indeed be foolishness, as is suggested. However lam quite ready to leave myself in your readers' bands and they shall judge. As far as lam con- cerned the matter is now ended, as I only wrote to ventilate what is after all a decided grievance to Warrington men, and not for notoriety. Again apologizing for taking up your valuable space.—l am, yours truly,

C. TURNER,

Fearnhead, Warrington, January 9th, 1907. [We shcmld like to add that Mr. Turner's lettar was written before the sad fatality to his young son at Padgate.—ED. "

CONGREGATIONAL GATHERINGS.

BOLD STREET 'WESLEYAN CHURCH. The annual congregational gathering in connection with Bold-street Wesleyan Church was held on Thursday night. The proceedings opened with a tea to which a large number sat down. Subsequently a concert was held in the schoolroom at which there was a good attendance, the Rev. J. Manning presiding. Musical items were given by Miss Cox, Miss Brough, Miss Barton, Miss E. Taylor, Miss Eckersley, Mr. Humphries and Mr. A. Dunn. Mr. Frank Hickman acted as accom- panist. Mr. LIGHT, secretary of the Sunday school, presented his annual report. They had to regret that many teachers had left them during the year and that they experienced some difficulty in getting new ones. Some 240 children had received instruction and the attendance averaged 90 in the morning and 170 in the afternoon. (Applause). The Rev. J. Manning in the course of a brief address, urged the members of the Church to keep up the interest in the old institutions. He was sure that the future of Methodism in Warrington depended very largely' upon the vigour and success of their Sunday Schools. His experience was that there were no workers in the Church like those whom they had trained themselves. (Hear, hear.) It would always be possible I when they were ready for it, occupying the position which they did, to crowd the Church but he was sure that the great difficulty would always be to secure workers. The cry at Bold-street in past years had been for \corker§ and teachers, especially for gentlemen teachers. The future would make greater demands upon them in the matter of rellgious educa- tion than the past had ever done. Anyone with their eyes open would see that the outcome of the religious controversy through which they were passing would be that in the near future there would be a greater tax and strain upon the resources of the Church to train the children in religion than ever before. Personally he would be extremely sorry too see the Bible shut out from the elementary schools of the land, though he could not shut his eyes to the fact that the outcome of the last two or three years discussion and attempt at compromise had brought the country much nearer to secular education. At least there was a growing opinion amongst the poople outside the churches and that was the only solution of the difficulty. He would be sorry to see that result as he could not con- ceive a complete scheme of education which did not involve the spiritual lives of the children.(Hear, hear.) I The work of physical and mental training could be safely left to the day schools but the Churches would have to pay increasing attention to the development of the morals ani character of the scholars. The hope of the future lay in the children and he trusted that the call for additional help and devoted work would be i heartily met, and that n days to come their Sunday School might rase up faithful workers for the Church. (Applause). PADGATE WESLEYAN CHURCH. Success attended the usual congregational tea meeting in connection with this church which was held on Thursday evening in the schoolroom. About 150 sat down to tea, thirty members of the congregation and friends having contributed trays. The public meeting which followed was presided Over by Mr. James Gandy and he was supported by the Rev. William Mellor, Mr. William Bennett, Messrs. H. Waid and F. Wright (superintendents of the Sunday school). Mr. J. JACKSON presented the annual report on the work of the Sunday school which stated that the year had been one of changes in connection with the work, but the secretaries were glad to report that the school was in a healthy condition. They took that opportunity of thanking their many supporters for their past help and hoped for a continuance of the same. There were 238 scholars on the books, with an average attendance of 78 in the morning and 159 in the afternoon. Ninety- seven of the scholars had gained prizes for regular and punctual attendance. The teachers and officers num- bered 27. A feeling allusion was made to the death of Councillor Brough who was the leader of the Young Men's Class and took a great interest in the work of the school. They would miss him very much for his devoted help and kindly encouragement and they extended deep sympathy with Mrs. Brough and family in their bereavement. The membership of the young men's class stood at 30 and there was an average attendance of 16. The committee had appointed Mr. James Gandy as teacher and Mr. W. E. Plinstoa as assistant teacher and they would be pleased to welcome new members. The treasurer commenced the year with a balance in hand of .£1 45.7 d. The receipts amounted to over £4O and the credit balance now stood at £2 3s. id. The officers for the year were :—Superintend- ents, Messrs. H. Ward and F. Wright ; treasurer, Mr. James Gaudy; auditor, Mr. Percy H. Bennett; secre- taries, Messrs. G. H. White and J. Jackson; club secre- taries and librarians, Messrs. L. Bennett, H. Brough,and S. Adamson. The CHAIRMAN congratulated the workers on the satisfactory state of the school. Remade a symathetic reference to the death of Councillor Brough, who was a familiar figure at those gatheripgs and one of their best supporters. The entertainment was then proceeded with and proved highly enjoyable. It included recitations by Annie Pover and -lames Mellor; songs by Miss Brown, Miss Dorothy Truslove' Mr. A. Greenough, Mr. L. Platt; duets by, Messrs. Greenough and Platt; violin solos by Miss Elsie Taylor, baloon drill by sixteen girls, musical recitation and chorus by May Dawson and girls, a dialogue by seven girls, and an action song by a contingent of scholars. All items were admirably interpretated and called forth the warmest approbation, encores being frequent. The children had been trained for the action songs by the Misses Ward and Miss E. London, and for the recitations by Miss London. During the evening the Rev. W. Mellor delivered a short address and spoke of the handsome addition which had been made to the church by the erection of a tower. He hoped that during the coming year they would be able to enlarge the school. (Applause.) The artistes and all who had contributed to the success of the gathering were thanked on the proposi- tion of Mr. Williem Bennett seconded by Mr. F. Wright.

THE FUTURE OF LABOUR.

TRADES COUNCIL TO BE RE-CONSTITUTED.

After many mouths waiting the' trade organizations ' affiliated with the Warrington Trades Council have announced their decisions with reference to .the pro-

posal that the labour forces of the town should be amalgamated. The question was first mooted as the result of a conference between the Trades Council, the local Labour Representation Committee and the Right to Work Committee at which it was unani• . mously agreed that for the more effective and economical working of the labour movement of War- rington the formation of one central organization was advisable. It was felt that there was a great and unnecessary wastage of funds and energy by the existence of separate labour authorities but some difficulty arose in regard to the constitution of the i proposed new. organization. In this the representa- Itires of the Labour Representation Committee took the lead and having a majority at the conference their view that the central body should be open to trade unions and all reform societies provided that they I were not connected with any political organiza- tion, was carried by a small majority. The , Trades Council delegates were not satisfied with ; this decision and at a meeting of the Executive Committee of the Counctl it was decided to refer the matter to the affiliated Branches to which the follow- ing questions were addressed: (1) Are you in favour of the Trades Council,the Labour Representation Com- mittee and the Right to Work Committee being disbanded and a new organisation formed on the lines of the National Labour Party, open to membership of trade union branches' socialist parties and co-opera- ative societies; (2) or do you prefer an organisation open to trade unions only ; (3) or are you in favour of the Trades Council remaining intact, withdrawing from the Labour Representation Committee and making such alteration in its rules as will' enable it to contest elections with trade union candidates ? These ques- tions were issued to the twenty-six trade unions connected with the Trades Council and prior to Tuesday nignt's meeting of the Council twenty- three replies had been received • The replies were: In favour of question No. 1 from the enginemen, mill-sawyers and machinists, iron founders, shop assistants, carpenters and joiners and cabinet workers (6) ; in favour of No. 2, the wire- drawers, cooch-builders, typograpical, painters and decorators and railway servants (5) ; and in favour of No. 3, the tailors, gas-workers and general labourers, assurance agents, railway servants, wiredrawers, bakers, , tramway workers, engineers, braziers and sheet metal workers general railway workers and wheelwrights and smiths (ll). The glass-workers declined to take any part. Mr. PLUMB, who presided, said the returns were to him unsatisfactory, as an equal number had voted for questions one and two as had voted for !question three.

Mr. SMITH, wiredrawer, asked for tie number of members represented by the different Branches. The wiredrawers who numbered 300 were not going to be " hum-drummed " by any small societies, the voting strength of which was perhaps ten or twenty. Mr. Smith's remark brought forth an indignant pro- test from the sepresentative of the sheet metal workers. Mr. Smith bad no right to talk that way. His Society was Absolutely unanimous in favour No. 3. At this stage the Council were in somewhat of a diffi- culty as to how to act,and their hesitation was increased by an explanation from Mr. Haley as to the position of the wiredrawers. In that Society, explained the repre- sentative, the vote had been taken by means of a ballot of the members, and the returns were 85 in favour of question No.l, 166 for the second and 94 for the third. His difficulty was that whilst the greatest number of replies had been in favour of question No. 2 the number in support was actually a minority of those who voted. The Council were wondering what to do when Coun- cillor Stott came to the rescue and proposed that enquiries based on questions Nos. 1 and 3 should again be sent to the Branches for their consideration. The engineers, whom he represented, were most strongly against any body being formed which included in its ranks Co-operative and Socialist Societies. Personally he was opposed to that decision because he did not believe that there would be any real or lasting benefit gained by trade union action alone. He however had to cip his duty to his Branch who had decided in favour of trade unionists standing alone, though he must say that he had found better labour men outside trade unions than many who called themselves trade unionists and he regretted very much that trade unions could not see their way to work with men who were doing more for the advancement of labour than a very large propor- tion of those who were numbered in the ranks of the unions.

Mr. GRELEY entered a protest against Councillor Stott saying there were better men outside trade unions than in. If a man could not see his way clear to join the society representing the trade at which he earned his living he could not be a very good socialist and personally he would not associate with such a man. (Hear, hear.) A somewhat desultory discussion arose as to the mean- ing of the questions and the conclusion was arrived at that all throe implied a change. In face of this Mr. Podmore argued that all who had voted desired a change and s the majority favoured the revision of the rules of the Trades Council be moved that question three be submitted to the Branches for their endorsement. This motion was approved.

NEW THEATRE SCHEMES. Latest Particulars.

As a result of the Christmas Day fire, Warrington is at present without its principal theatre and as an almost natural consequence rumour is rife in our midst. Dating almost from the time of the accidental destrac- tien of Warrington's chief play house it has been freely whispered that we are to have a new and up-to- date theatre but if all the persons whose names have been associated with the ventur were to commence operations Warrington's tastes would be catered for by at least half-a-dozen palatial structures. The desire for prophecy has spread like an infection and has even involved the daily press. On Thursday a certain publication announced in the stop press column that a new theatre was to be constructed on the site of the Theatre Royal,Scotland- road. Later the error was realized and the next day another plunge was made in a joint newspaper in which it was backed by a similar statement appearing ia the whole of the Liverpool and Manchester press. " The land" it was confidently announced upon which the Royal Court Theatre stood 0 0 0 is about to become the property of Moss's Empire's Limited who it is stated intend to shortly commence the erection of a new music hall." It is almost unnecessary to deny such an idle assertion for, as we announced on December 29th, the proprietors of the Royal Court Theatre themselves intend to re-build. As the result of investigation, we are, however, able to announce that there is some truth in regard to the rumour that new theatre proprietors are to establish themselves in the town. Some five years ago a plot of land in the rear of Bridge-street,behind the Feathers Hotel, was acquired on a -lease from the Corporation on which the lessees announced that they proposed to erect a first-class theatre. The site has much to commend it, not the least being its centrality, whilst there is ample space-7,500 square feet—for the erection of a handsome building. The venture, however, fell through. Quite recently the site has changed hands, and whilst we have no official informs- tton, we understand that it will shortly be occupied by a music hall with a main entrance in Barbauld-street., the promoters of the project having even ventured to state that it will be opened on August Monday. We have reason to believe that it will be run on modern lines with two shows a night. Yesterday our representative waited upon Mr. Joseph Potter by whom he was informed that the work of clearing the Royal Court Theatre site prior to re- construction had already commenced. Asked as to the intention of the proprietors Mr. Potter said it had been decided to utilize the existing walls and stage but to re model the interior by contructing two tiers and increasing the height. The entrance hall will also be re-con-structed and modernized. It is intented to re-open the premises on August Monday.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."]

Will Warrington get Sam Johnson of Leigh? It was known on Christmas Day that Leigh were willing to open negotiations and the Warrington Committee were Informed of the fact. Now it is announced that Johnson, Bennett and. Silcock are on the transfer list, and there should be some competition for the men, especially the first-named. He is a really good wing three-quarter and would be invaluable to Warrington at the present time. The Leigh Club is hard up and endeavouring to" raise the wind" by a sale of stock. Warrington Football Club is well-off and ought to buy if prices are all right.

There was nothing very thrilling about Saturday's match at Warrington,the men from St. Helens proving easy victims as expected. Though only leading by six points to three points at half-time,and a man short for the rest of the game, the home team's superiority was unmistakable and finally the score had grown to 20 points. Once again there was a fine attendance, and there is no questioning the fact that the " Wire- pullers " have nothing to complain about in the way of public support, whatever may be the experience else- where. If the Northern Union is to eventually go down the Warrington Club will be one of the last to keep the flag flying.

In comparison with the titanic struggle against Runcorn on New Year's Day the St. Helens match seemed very tame. It was obvious that the home players took things pretty easily,and if the Saints could have scored first it would have acted as a fine tonic. However, they contrived to make the position inter- esting before half-time. Warrington were soon two tries up as the result of clever work by Isherwood and Dickinson. Unfortunately the latter got damaged and had to retire in order to nurse a strained leg. After this the Saints bustled about to some purpose and rubbed off half the deficiency. They should, in fact, have been within a point of Warrington at the interval for Toole had the easiest of goal-kicks and only succeeded in shaking the upright—much to his disgust.

There was a more business-like air about the home lot on resuming. They recognised that, handicapped by the absence of Dickenson, they would have to exert themselves to secure the verdict. Fortunately the five remaining forwards proved capable of holding the opposition and the ball came pretty regularly into the hands of the Warrington halves. The latter soon opened the game out, and Isherwood capped a fine run with a try near the corner. Meanwhile Thomas was burning to distinguish himself as a wing player. His chance soon came, for Isherwood replied to a penalty kick with a dodgy run down touch and a pass to Thomas. The latter shouldered his way over and emerged from a heap of the slain with the smile that " wouldn't come off." St. Helens were quite conquered by this time and Lees and Harmer got further tries, Preston kicking his only goal at the last attempt.

Probably the most pleasing feature of the match from a Warrington point of view was the return to form of Isherwood. He ran brilliantly on Saturday and played a perfect centre game, four of the six tries coming as the results of his efforts. With Dickenson fit-and in such fine trim the question is what tn do with Ike Taylor, who is also expected to be fit for duty very soon. He is too good a player to languish in the A "team and in my opinion he should have a trial at half-back. The present pair are smart, but bath are on the light side whereas Taylor has the necessary physique, and dash. He might just make the man Warrington have been wanting for years. With three good centres—and two capable reserves on the "A "team —something should be done to make full use of their services. Not one of them would be a success on the wing, but there is sufficient similarity between half and centre play to make the experiment worth trying.

Tilley played a much better game than on Nev Year's Day, and he has proved a most useful man at a critical juncture. With Isherwood, Fish, and Jordan off the other day, the left wing would have been in Queer-street but for the Widnes man. Ile Is not fast enough for threequarter play and the sooner Fish and Jordan are available once more and the better. Berry is playing magnificently at full back and seems to have benefitted by the competition for his position which was threatened. For the last three or four matches Brooks has. been under a cloud and nary a try has come his way. Yet he has played with plenty of dash. Luck has a lot to do with it —and the fact that Dickenson has been getting the tries himself. The latter was never a good man for his wing, and in that one respect is inferior to Isherwood and Taylor.

St. Helens played a batter game than the score would suggest. In fact the backs passed very prettil, but it was too much across the fibld. If they had a centre who would go through occasionally they would make a much nalre dangerous side. The Creevys played smartly, but they bungled a glorious chance on the right once when they had Berry between them. Hesitation proved fatal and lost a certain try. The home full back cleared with a sort of "run-through shot" which brought down the house. Toole was good at half and tried hard. to improve the position of matters, while the veteran full back, Atkinson, could not be blamed for any of the tries scored against him.

Just now they are travelling in remarkable fashion and look like maintaining the lead with ease.

PROPOSED PUBLIC CONVENIENCE IN BUTTERNIARKEr-STREET.

RESIDENTS' opposmoN.

A long discussion took place with reference to the minute of the Paving and Sewerage Committee "That a urinal be erected in Dial-street on the site of the Old Bridewell." A plan was shown, and some details given by Alderman Evans. A petition, signed by 26 rfwmirs and occupiers of the properties in the immediate neighbourhood, opposing the proposal "for reasous of health, morality, and public decency," was read. Alderman EVANS, in proposing the confirmation of the minute, said he understood the neighbourhood was up in arms against the proposal. Proceeding, he urged that the convenience was an absolute necessity. Councillor CLEGG led the opposition. The site in question, he said, was a valuable one, and why should it be utilized for such a minor concern ? It was a hare- brained scheme to utilize one of the most valuable sites in the borough for such an abominable purpose. He had never come across anything that had been so ill-considered since he had been a member of the Council, and he moved as an amendment that the resolu- tion be referred back to the committee for considera- tion.

Councillor CH A.NTLER seconded. • Alderman BENNETT said be did not think he had ever heard a proposal put forward with less to recommend it. They ought not to ignore the memorial which had been read, but it was almost an affront to the improv- ing tastes of the people of the town to endeavour to put their open spaces to such a use. It was suggested that they should erect a monument here in memory of John Howard, a very famous Englishman, who did much of his best work in Warrington in the matter of the reform of prisons. It was already called "Howard Place."

Alderman TINNION in supporting the minute said he thought some people were a little over squeamish in the matter. There was the same objection wherever they chose a site. Councillor WILDING urged that the site be used for something more ornamental. _ _ _ _

Councillor-NIELD suggested that the question should be re-considered at the next monthly meeting, when they, had had an opportunity of consulting their constituents.

In answer to Councillor Pemberton,

Alderman EVANS said the cost of the urinal would be about £lBO. He added that a nuisance was created on the open space by boys at play. The other day no fewer than 50 of them were playing at football with tin cans —(laughter)—and the inhabitants complained that they "could not hear their own ears."

Councillor CLEGG observed that there were 14 public conveniences within a radius of a mile of the spot in question. The amendment of Councillor Clegg, altered to read that the minute on page 12, relating to the urinal be not confirmed. The voting was as follows:

FoR THE AM END M ENT.- AGAINST. Alderman Bennett Alderman Tinnion

VACANCIES ON COMMITTEES.

Councillor STOTT drew attention to the minute of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee appointing Councillor Wilkinson a member in place of the late Councillor Brough. He understood that Councillor Wilkinson was already on seven committees—the maximum number allowed by the Standing Orders. Had he taken steps to resign from one of them? He (Conn- cillor Stott), moved that the minute be not coarmei. Councillor LORD seconded the amendment, and said some members of the Council were only on one com- mittee, besides which he did not think the vacancy ought to be filled until the late Councillor Beugh's successor was elected. Councillor WILKINSON: The minute certainly reflects very great credit upon Councillor Wilkinson. Continuing he said he had previously withdrawn from the committee.and hehad beeniproposed again unknown to himself. He thought the Standing Order obnoxious, as it prevented a member who was willing to work from doing more than a certain amount. It was decided to adhere to the Standing Order and Councillor Wilkinson being therefore ineligible Coun- cillor Cunningtoa was appointed to fill the vacancy.

Cricket.

There is every possibility of there being a 3 unior Cricket League for Warrington next season. No doubt many people have thought that one should have been formed before now, but the lack of support would not allow it. During last season the game underwent great change and several of the leading local clubs have decided to band together and do their utmost to form the league. With such clubs as Orford Barracks, Cheshire Lines, Latchford Baptists, Bewsey Wesleyans Guardian, Cable Works and others banding together a good fixture list is promised.

A meeting is to be held on Tuesday evening, the 15th January, at the Cocoa Rooms, 119, Sankey-street. when such club as are interested in the above may send two representatives.

===== 0002601_19070112_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. WEEK OF UNITED PRAYER.

Free Church Councils all over the country are this week holding united prayer meetings in the different centres. In Warrington an interesting series of meet- ings has been held in the leading Nonconformist Churches, and topics of great interest discussed.

. TEMPERANCE. - The opening meeting was held on Monday evening in the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church. The Rev. J. Manning presided over a small attendance. Mr. C. E. PARKER, President of the Warrington Council, delivered an'address on Temperance. He did not think, he said, it was necessary for him to enlarge upon the havoc which intemperance was making in the material, social, and spiritual well-being of the people I of England, and they all knew only too well the ' slow progress which they were making towards, the ideal of a sober and righteous nation. it had come almost as a surprise to them that there was an indication of a diminution in the annont of intoxicating liquor drunk in the country and they scarcely dared to hope that that indication was evidence of a permanent change in the habits of the people. The direction hitherto taken by temper- ance effort was towards the curtailment of facilities for drinking, and in the influencing of individuals, or generally, in giving attention to the results of drinking. He would like to direct their thoughts to another side of the question which had received too little attention from temperance workers—the contributory causes which made for drunkenness. The principal of these were, in women ill-health, and in men unemployment. It behoved them in their labours on behalf of temperance to pay greater attention to the condition of the victims of drink—the question of unemployment, and interlocked with that the question of the housing and recreation of the masses of the people. (Hear, hear.)

"UNITY AMONG CHRISTIANS.' On Tuesday evening a meeting was held in the Bold- street Church Parlour, Mr. G. Henshall presiding over a fair attendance. The speaker was the Rev. J. Yonge, the subject dealt with being" Unity among Christians." The Rev. J. YONGE in the course of an interesting address said it should be an inspiration to those present to know that they were part and parcel of a great host that no man could number. Christians of all races and peoples were meeting together in that the first week of the new year with the one object of worshipping Jesus Christ, who had His followers in every land—in Central Africa, in the vast areas of China, in our great Empire of India. They sometimes had differences, each having their own idea as to how tho churches should be governed, but now they had met together on the, broad platform of dieipleship of Christ, and one was charmed with a sense of unity. If they were to have that, they must look beyond thp limits orthe bounds of their own particular church. 'lt should be their greatest privilege to Point out to men their salvation. They must not forget the supreme aim of Christianity, which was to effect the salvation of souls through Christ's love. Christ's prayer that they should love one another should be put into practical operation. They would realize something of that prayer whorl they entered more fully into Christ's words : " Whatsoever ye shall ask in My name that will I give unto you."

"True Imperialism." On Wednesday evening the Rev. F. Vaughan, addressed a meeting in Bewsey-road Lecture Hall, on "True Imperialism " and remarked that this call to Prayer and the suggested topic led to the question "How far had Christ realised Himself and His Divine Mission in the nations after 1900 years of Christian enterprize ?" Certain recent events, pointed to the existence of fearful evils and gave rise to some pessi- mism as to the progress of the Gospel and the establish- ment of the Kingdom of Heaven upon earth. The speaker alluded to the warlike temperament of the nation who had appealed to the world for" Peace," near home. The Peace proclamation in South Africa ushered in strife in the realm of Africa's fields of labour. While English homes had been aroused by bitter controversy on religious questions. The terrible revelations of the Sweating Exhibition, the Congo atrociters, of the liquor and gambling evils, together with the degradation of national sport to serve the greed for gold, gave use to the fear that the pission for power and the love of money, would make the nation forget her debt to the God of Power, and the needs of suffering humanity. The pathetic appeals of the heathen world for light and leading, and th 3 methods now necessarily adopted by the Arnbissadors of the Christ to catch the eye and ear of the masses of the people,showed the deep need there was for penitence and prayer by the most favoured nation the world had ever seen.

The speaker declared true Imperialism to be somAhing more than the opening of new shops and wider markets, rather the purity of an exalted people who by regeneration and grace were partakers of the thoughts and purposes of God, occupying itself with the build- ing of character rather than the construction of armaments, and who, for Christ's sake gave forth its energy to protect the weak, stimulate the fainting and defend the right. This was the heritage of the nation, when surrendered to the gospel call because it was the birthright of each individual who had been led into fellowship with Christ. Thore was much reason for hope. The enterprise %which called that gathering together was an evidence that behind what appeared on the surface of things was a Living Person calling His own followers to renewed energy and more lofty aspirations. And that Person was the God who clothed the hard uninviting earth with beauty, and transformed Peter from a handful of sand to the Pentacostal Rock he proved himself to be in Jerusalem, 1000 years ago God was on the side of all good men and would surely prevail. As to the methods by which the Divine Purpose was to be brought about he reminded them that since each unit of the Christian Church was a temple, a dvveliing place of the truer God, individual consecration and co-operation was the only way we needed, more faith in the living Church, more faith in eaih other, a clearer realisation of the sacredness of life, of brotherhood, and of Christian obligations.

LIBERAL WARD MEETINGS. St. JW—i-i-ii Ward.

The annual meeting of the Liberal Committee of St. John's Ward was held in Brick-street Mission on Monday evening. Mr. W. Furness, J.P., presiding over a good attendance, which included Hr. Joseph Efowes and Mr. Joseph Howes, jun. Votes of thanks were passed to the retiring officers, and congratulations were extended to Mr. Furness and Hr. J. Maginn on their elevation to the magisterial Bench. Mr. Furness was re-elected chairman of the commitsee, and Mr. J. Rudd was re-elected general secretary.—The Chairman thanked the members for their congratulations and said it was largely owing to his interest in St. John's Ward that he had been appointed to the office of magistrate. Mr. J. Howes delivered an address expressing his great gratification at the enthusiasm displayed and urged the workers to do their utmost to retain the seat which had been so nobly won for Liberalism. He congratulated the committee on having sues an excellent chairman.

The annual meeting of the Orford Ward Committee was held in the Silver-street Schools last evening, Councillor Wilkinson, J.P., presiding over a good attendance. LAMPLIGHTERS' WAGES.

The Gas Committee recommend that the wages of lamplighters be increasect to 2-Is, per week, to Include Saturday and Sunday work. Councillor STOTT said the men were entitled under a resotuion passed some time ago 255. per week. It had been decided to pay them 21s. for their week's work up to Saturday noon, and time and a half for their work from Saturday noon to Monday morning, an increase of is. 9d. a week. He contended the men should have that increase without working extra hours and ho hoped the Chairman would withdraw the minute.

The MAICOR said he was informed by the Gas Manager that the men were perfectly satisfied with the present arrangement. Councillor STOrr said that was news to him, and be was relucant to interfere between the management and the men.

- Alderman EvANS said if he remembered rightly what was now proposed was what the men requested. They objected to being paid time and a half from Saturday noon till Monday morning. THE SHIP CANAL. TO ACQUIRE LAND._

The TOWN CLERK reported to the Rivers Committee that the Ship Canal Co., proposed to acquire lands in Arpley and had given notice of their intention to obtain Parliamentary powers for that purpose. Alderman BENS= moving the adoption of th 9 minutes, said he supposed the Canal Company wanted land for the disposal of the spoil dredged from the river. He hoped the spoil would not be an encumbrance to future projects. He often regretted that the Arpley meadows were being rendered impracticable as a site for a dock. Councillor FULTON-SMITH seconded, and the minutes were agreed to. CONGRATULATIONS. _ _

KILLED AT PLAY.

I Sad Fatality at Padgate

A distressing fatality occurred at Padgate on Wednes- day when, during play time in the afternoon, ono of the scholars, Thomas Turner (7), was run over and killed. The circumstances of the accident were revealed to Mr. S. Brighouse and a jury at an inquest held at the Stocks Inn last evening. Mr. Turner, School House, Fearnhead, headmaster at St. Barnabas school, Warrington,said he last saw the deceased alive at 5.15 on Wednesday morning. He heard later that he had been killed. Thomas Coulon,driver for Mr. Ed ward Bennett,farmer, Padgate, said that on Wednesday he was driving a horse and float and was returning to the farm. He was com- ing past the school about three o'clock. There were a number of children playing and the deceased was on the footpath. Two or three lads were swinging at the back of the float and he had turned to speak to them when deceased ran on to the road in front of the float. Witness tried to pull up, but was unable to do so, and the right shaft hit deceased and knocked him down, the wheel of the float passing over him. He carried the boy to Mr. Blackwell, the schoolmaster, and afterwards took him to a doctor who pronounced life to beextinct. Deceased never moved after the accident. Witness had 'been driving seven years and the pony was a quiet and steady I one.

At this stage Mr. Brighouse read a statement written by Mr. Trrner, which ha-said reflected great credit upon him. Mr. Turner said :"I have had various suggestions thrown out to the effect that blame ought to be attached in various places.l wish to state publicly that I dislike such insinuations and I personally attach no blame in any quartei. As a child, I took the same risks in the same place. Since then probably over a thousand different children have done so and as long as boys are boys such risks must be,even with every precaution possible. I look upon the tragedy as a pure accident and simply feel that my boy was wanted for work elsewhere and so got promotion early on. As a schoolmaster I wish to express my sympathy with Mr. and Mrs. tslackewell, knowing as I do how I should feel such an accident in my own school. As parents both Mrs. Turner and myself beg to give grateful thanks to the Irishman who had the misfortune to drive the horse, to Mr. Blakwell and Mr. Dennett for the efforts they made to preserve my boy's life and to assure them I shall never forget their great kianess." The wry returned a verdict of, "Accidental aeath," and added that no blame attached to anyone. They recommended that until a new playground was provided a teacher or teachers should keep an eye on the children managers, Th The R . when at phsy. e‘xVp.rePsosleldockii-yHmipall, tbc of the school yhairman with Mr. and Mrs. Turner in their bereavement. He explained that the managers had just completed negotiations for the exten- sion of the playground, and that this would be done immediately. - , Mr. Bennett also expressed his sympathy with Mr.. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe Church. New Years's Sunday. Rev. John Yonge and Sunday Observance. New Year's Sunday was observed at Wycliffe Church Iby the holding of spdcial services. There were large congregations. In the morning there was a young people's service, when the Rev. Alfred A. Lee, of Emmanuel Church, Bootle,delivered a pOwerful address on the words: "We ought to obey God rather than man "(Acts v., 29). At the close the annual presenta- tion of Bibles to the nsembers of the pastor's class.took place. Thirty-two young ,peoplo received Bibles making ; a total since 1881 of 735. 1 At the P.S.A, meeting in the afternoon the President ; (Rev. J. Yonge) addressed a large assembly on "A New I Year and a new start." He said the end of a year I suggested review and over-hauling and seeing how things stood. All sound businessmen did • that and the same method should be adopted by Christians in 'the business of life. That was the supreme business—their life, and what to do with and in it; its days, its powers, and opportunities. God

British Women's Temperance Association. A meeting of the above Society was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Church Parlour, Bold-street. ;Mts. Owen, of Penketh, presided. Arrangements • were made for a visit from Mrs. Frank-Coop, of Southport, to give at the next meeting a report of her visit to tke Boston Convention. Mrs. Holmes, of Sankey, who is. President of the Association sent £2 to be used in Temperance work along with the message "Do not lose your zeal, go on with the good work, for everything is with you." The Secretary, Mrs. Simpson, passed to the members a book which ghe had received from Mr. Charles E. 'Parker, of Penketh. In commenting upon the Work, the Secre- tary said, "It is written by Mr. Parker and is a memoir of his mother. It is a record of a well-spent life and Might to be read by every "British Woman " for -on the 21st of April, 1876, Mrs. Margaret E. Parker was the means of forming the British Women's Temperance Association in the United Kingdom and was elected first Presicienrof the Society. She worked strenuously for the Temperance cause and for the emancipation of women from the trammels of custom "

FOOTBALL. NORTHERN RUGBY LEAGUE. , RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S MATCEIES. G. T. P. G. T..P Leeds 2 3 13 Oldham 1 1 5 Hunslet 1 2 8 Wakefield T. ... 0 1 3 Hull Warrington Huddersfield Brad ford Halifax Keighley Rochdale Hornets 0 2 6 Batley Salford 3 7 27 Swinton 0 0 0 Barrow 413 47 Liverpool City 0 1 3 Warrington v. St. Helens. The Warrington men were at home again on Saturday, St.Helens furnishing the opposition. Unfortunately the ground tai not recovered from the effects of snow and rain and proved very sticky in the centre. Both sides were well represented and a well-contested game was the result, St. Helens making a better show than the score would suggest. Teams : Warrington---Berry, back; Brooks, Dickinson, Isher- wood and Tilley, three-quarter backs; Lees and Jenkins, half backs; Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Shugars and Harmer, forwards. . St. Helens—Atkinson, back re. Creevey, J. Creevy, Hilton, and Barton, three-quarter backs; Toole and M, Creevy, half backs; Briers,M.ooney, Whiteley, Wharton, Smith, and Pope. forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Asquith, Wakefield ; touch judges Messrs. C. Platt (Swinton) and E. Williams (Widnes). The game opened directly in favour of Warrington, and smart play between Isherwood and Tilley ended in a score to the latter. Warrington again pressed, and though Atkinson staved off the pressure for a time the locals would not be denied. Dickenson made a great single-handed effort which led up to Isherwood scoring. Unfortunately Dickenson was hurt, and took no further part in the match. St. Helens pressed, and a long dribble by Whiteley gave Pope an opportunity to score in a good position, Toole's shot at goal hitting the posts. Half-time : Warrington, 6 points ; St. Helens, 3 points. On resuming with Thomas on the left wing, and , Dickenson still absent, Warrington set to work with determination. The position admitted of no slackness, and the forwards holding their own the superi- ority of the men behind soon told a tale. Lees and Jenkins repeatedly opened out the game and a brilliant effort by Isherwood gave Warrington a further lead. The veteran home eentre was to the fore a minute later, for a tricky run down the left provided. Thomas with an opening to- score at the corner. St. Helens rallied, but Berry easily drove them back. Renewing the attack Lees crossed in rather clever fashion, whilst neit the close misfielding let in Harmer, who scored near the posts for Preston to land a goal. Result :—Warrington 1. goal, 6 tries (20 points), St. Heleas 1 try (3 points). RUNCORN v. HALIFAX. This postponed League match attracted about 2,000 spectators at Runcorn on Tuesday, considerable interest being manifested owing to the fine performance of both teams this season. Unfortunatly for Runcorn Jolley was absent, whilst Brearley replaced Bulmer in the Halifax pack. The ground was in splendid condition. Teams : Runcorn—Harrison back; Deakin, Butterworth, Moran, and FlanaganAhree-quarter backs; Walton and Richardson, half backs; Walker, Padbury, Williams, Dixon, Leylands, and Dobson, forwards. Hal ifa x—Li ttl e,back; William s,Wedgewood,Williams, and Eccles, three-quarter backs ; Riley and Hilton, half , backs; Bartle Littlewood, Brearley, Morley, Robinson, ' and Swinbank, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. Robinson, Manningham. Touch inudges Messrs. E. Bennett (Liverpool) and J. H. Houghton (St. Helens). In the opening stages Little was robbed of possession by Deakin, who scored, Richardson failing at goal. Hilton broke away with a smart run,gaining considerable ground. In quick- succession Wedgewood placed two goals, giving Halifax the lead. Walton got away, but , held the ball too long. Wedgeweod added a further I goal before the interval. Half-time score :—Halifax 31 goals (6 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points). On resuming Moran missed a fine chance of scoring through failing to catch the bill. Harrison when returning the ball had his kick charged by Morley, who got possession and scored for Wedgewood to add another goal. Deakin got hold in the Halifax half, and raced over at the corner,Richardson failing to augment. Near the close Eccles ran through the home backs and scored a capital try, Wedgewood improving. The open style of play made the game decidedly interesting, and Halifax fully deserved their success, through seizing every opportunity, also by their superior kicking, Wedge wood placing five goals. Final score :—Hal i fax 5 goals and 2 tries (16 points), Runcorn 2 tries 6 points). LANCASNIRE COMBINATION. Sr. HELENS "A " v. WArenr,....rox " A."—This return was play ea at St. Helens on Saturday. Warrington opened well and Jones scored a fine try in the first few minutes, as the result of excellent passing. The visitors still maintained the aggressive, and Hewitt scored an unimproved try, whilst near the interval St. Helens got over. Turning round Warrington again had the better of the game and scored two unconverted tries, whilst St. Helens added three points. lainal : Warrington, 15 points ; St. Helens, 6 points. G. T. P. G. T. P. Runcorn A Wigan .. RUGBY UNION. _— STOCKTON HEATH -V, TRAFFORD ROVERS.—Played at Stockton Heath on Saturday, before a capital attend- ance. The home side had the advantage; of- the breeze during the first half, and Steele's initial kick was followed by a prolonged attack. From some smart work Kirkham scored an unconverted try. The Rovers rallied only to find the Stockton Heath defence sound and renewing the attack again Langley scored. Steele missing the goal. Interval : Stockton Heath, two tries (six points) ; Trafford Rovers, nil. In the second half the leaders pressed their aggressive tactics,and had matters much as they pleased. Langley scored three tries, and Jones and Smith once each, Steele landing one goal. Final: Stockton Heath, 26 points ; TralTord Rovers, five points. Stockton Heath " A," 29 points ;Sale " B," nil. Sankey St. Mary's 1, Widnes St. Paul's 2. 411> Death of Mrs. Carter, Bridge street. We record with regret the death which took place at her residence, of Mrs. Elizabeth Carter, one of the oldest shopkeepers in the town. Mrs. Carter, who was in her 73rd year, had carried on the business of a jeweller in Bridge-street for a large number of years. She was only ill for a short time, and finally succumbed to acute bronchitis. She leaves a sister, a son, and two grandchildren to mourn her loss. The funeral took place at Hill Cliffe grave-yard on Monday after- noon. The chief mourners were Mr. Thomas Armstrong Carter (son), the Rev. J. Tesseyman and Mr. Woods (brothers-in-law), Mr. William Isaac (nephew), and Mr. Kenworthy. The service was conducted by the Rev. John Tesseyman, of Porters Heath, near Bristol, deceased's brotEer-in-law. A large number of wreaths and floral tributes were receiv ed. The arrangements were carried out by Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons.
